\displaystyle\leq E_{r}\Bigl(n,\varepsilon,K_{k}\bigl\|\breve{L}\bigl(\upsilon_{k}^{(1)},\dotsc,\upsilon_{k}^{(n)}\bigr)\bigr\|_{n,r^{\prime}}\Bigr)

(167)

for every
n>k
and
\varepsilon>0
, where the second equality is again obtained using Lemma
7.7
. Now by (
165
) and Hölder’s inequality, which ensures that
\|{\cdot}\|\equiv\|{\cdot}\|_{2}\leq(k+1)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{r}}\,\|{\cdot}\|_{r}
on
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
,
we have

\displaystyle\breve{L}_{k}(n):=\bigl\|\breve{L}\bigl(\upsilon_{k}^{(1)},\dotsc,\upsilon_{k}^{(n)}\bigr)\bigr\|_{n,r^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq L\biggl(1+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\upsilon_{k}^{(i)}\|^{r}\biggr)^{1/r^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,r}L\,\Biggl\{1+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\,\bigl(\bigl|h_{i}^{\ell}\bigr|^{r}+\lvert\gamma_{i}\rvert^{r}\bigr)\Biggr\}^{1/r^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=L\biggl(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\,\bigl(\|h^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r}+\|\gamma\|_{n,r}^{r}\bigr)\biggr)^{1/r^{\prime}}

(168)

for each
n>k
. Since
\|h^{\ell}\|_{n,r}=O_{c}(1)
for
1\leq\ell\leq k
by
\mathcal{H}_{k}(b)
and
\|\gamma\|_{n,r}=O_{c}(1)
by
(A1)
, this means that
\breve{L}_{k}(n)=O_{c}(1)
. Thus, by Proposition
1.2
, there exists
\bar{L}_{k}\in(0,\infty)
such that for integers
n>k
, the events
A_{k}(n):=\{\breve{L}_{k}(n)\leq\bar{L}_{k}\}\in\mathscr{S}_{k}(n)
satisfy
\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(A_{k}(n)^{c}\bigr)<\infty
. Moreover, for each
n>k
and
\varepsilon>0
, it follows from (
167
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{\lvert T_{n1}^{\prime}\rvert>\varepsilon\}\cap A_{k}(n)\!\bigm|\!\mathscr{S}_{k}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}(\lvert T_{n1}^{\prime}\rvert>\varepsilon\,|\,\mathscr{S}_{k})\mathbbm{1}_{A_{k}(n)}

\displaystyle\leq P\bigl(n,\varepsilon,\upsilon_{k}^{(1)},\dotsc,\upsilon_{k}^{(n)}\bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{A_{k}(n)}

\displaystyle\leq E_{r}\bigl(n,\varepsilon,K_{k}\breve{L}_{k}(n)\bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{A_{k}(n)}\leq E_{r}(n,\varepsilon,K_{k}\bar{L}_{k}),

where we have used the fact that
A_{k}(n)\in\mathscr{S}_{k}(n)
to obtain the first equality above. Recalling the expression for
E_{r}(n,\varepsilon,K_{k}\bar{L}_{k})
in (
166
), we see that
\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}E_{r}(n,\varepsilon,K_{k}\bar{L}_{k})<\infty
, and hence conclude as in (
151
) that for every
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\lvert T_{n1}^{\prime}\rvert>\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\{\lvert T_{n1}^{\prime}\rvert>\varepsilon\}\cap A_{k}(n)\bigr)+\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(A_{k}(n)^{c}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}E_{r}(n,\varepsilon,K_{k}\bar{L}_{k})+\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(A_{k}(n)^{c}\bigr)<\infty,

which implies (
164
) in view of Proposition
1.2
. Together with (
162
) and (
163
), this shows that
T_{n1}\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi(\bar{G}_{1},\dotsc,\bar{G}_{k},\bar{G}_{k+1},\bar{\gamma})\bigr)
in (
161
), as claimed.

The final step in the proof of
\mathcal{H}_{k+1}(c)
is to show that
T_{n2}\overset{c}{\to}0
in (
161
). Letting
L>0
be such that
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{k+2}(r,L)
, we can apply Lemma
7.24
as in (
152
) to see that

\displaystyle\lvert T_{n2}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\bigl|\psi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},h_{i}^{k+1,k},\gamma_{i})-\psi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},h_{i}^{k+1,k}-\Delta_{i}^{k+1},\gamma_{i})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq L(k+2)^{\frac{r}{2}-1}\,\|\Delta^{k+1}\|_{n,r}\biggl(1+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\|h^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r-1}+2\|\gamma\|_{n,r}^{r-1}+\|h^{k+1,k}\|_{n,r}^{r-1}+\|h^{k+1,k}-\Delta^{k+1}\|_{n,r}^{r-1}\biggr)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,r}L\|\Delta^{k+1}\|_{n,r}\biggl(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\|h^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r-1}+\|h^{k+1,k}\|_{n,r}^{r-1}+\|\gamma\|_{n,r}^{r-1}+\|\Delta^{k+1}\|_{n,r}^{r-1}\biggr)

(169)

for each integer
n>k
. Now
\|h^{k+1,k}\|_{n,r}\overset{d}{=}\|h^{k+1}\|_{n,r}
for each such
n
, and recall from
\mathcal{H}_{k+1}(a)
that
\|\Delta^{k+1}\|_{n,r}=o_{c}(1)
and from
\mathcal{H}_{k+1}(b)
that
\|h^{\ell}\|_{n,r}=O_{c}(1)
for
1\leq\ell\leq k+1
. Thus,
T_{n2}\overset{c}{\to}0
by (
169
), and we conclude from (
161
) that
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},h_{i}^{k+1},\gamma_{i})\overset{d}{=}T_{n}=T_{n1}+T_{n2}\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi(\bar{G}_{1},\dotsc,\bar{G}_{k},\bar{G}_{k+1},\bar{\gamma})\bigr)
, as required.

\mathcal{H}_{k+1}(d)
: The arguments in this proof are similar to those given for
\mathcal{H}_{1}(d)
and
\mathcal{H}_{k+1}(c)
, so we outline the key steps without going into the full details. By (
160
) and Lemma
7.6
(c), we have
(\gamma,m^{0},h^{1},\dotsc,h^{k},h^{k+1})\mathrel{\overset{d}{=}\!|_{\mathscr{S}_{k}}}(\gamma,m^{0},h^{1},\dotsc,h^{k},h^{k+1,k})
for each
n>k
, so for fixed
\phi\in\mathrm{PL}_{k+2}(1)
, it follows that
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}^{0}\,\phi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},h_{i}^{k+1})\overset{d}{=}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}^{0}\,\phi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},h_{i}^{k+1,k})=:T_{n}
for each such
n
. Using (
160
), we now write

\displaystyle T_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}^{0}\,\phi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},\tilde{h}_{i}^{k+1},\gamma_{i})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}^{0}\bigl\{\phi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},h_{i}^{k+1,k},\gamma_{i})-\phi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},\tilde{h}_{i}^{k+1},\gamma_{i})\bigr\}

\displaystyle=:T_{n1}+T_{n2}

(170)

for each
n>k
, and aim to prove that
T_{n1}\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(F_{0}(\bar{\gamma})\cdot\phi(\bar{G}_{1},\dotsc,\bar{G}_{k},\bar{\gamma})\bigr)
and
T_{n2}\overset{c}{\to}0
, which together imply the desired conclusion.

For
T_{n1}
, recall once again from (
143
) or (
160
) that
\tilde{h}^{k+1}(n)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\bar{\alpha}_{\ell}^{k}\,h^{\ell}(n)+\overset{\raisebox{-0.7pt}{\scalebox{0.7}[0.35]{$\perp$}}}{\tau}_{k+1}\tilde{Z}^{k+1}(n)
for each
n
, where
\tilde{Z}^{k+1}\equiv\tilde{Z}^{k+1}(n)\sim N_{n}(0,I_{n})
is independent of
\mathscr{S}_{k}\equiv\mathscr{S}_{k}(n)=\sigma(\gamma,m^{0},h^{1},\dotsc,h^{k})
. Define
\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{k+1}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\Phi(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k},y):=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\phi\bigl(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k},\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\bar{\alpha}_{\ell}^{k}\,x_{\ell}+\overset{\raisebox{-0.7pt}{\scalebox{0.7}[0.35]{$\perp$}}}{\tau}_{k+1}Z,y\bigr)\bigr\}
with
Z\sim N(0,1)
.
Then
\Phi\in\mathrm{PL}_{k+1}(1)
by Lemma
7.23
(b), and as in (
162
) and (
163
), it follows from the inductive hypothesis
\mathcal{H}_{k}(d)
and Lemma
7.7
that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}^{0}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(\phi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},\tilde{h}_{i}^{k+1},\gamma_{i})\!\bigm|\!\mathscr{S}_{k}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}^{0}\,\Phi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},\gamma_{i})

\displaystyle\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(F_{0}(\bar{\gamma})\cdot\Phi(\bar{G}_{1},\dotsc,\bar{G}_{k},\bar{\gamma})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl(F_{0}(\bar{\gamma})\cdot\phi(\bar{G}_{1},\dotsc,\bar{G}_{k},\bar{G}_{k+1},\bar{\gamma})\bigr).

(171)

Next, we show as in (
164
) that

T_{n1}^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}^{0}\bigl\{\phi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},\tilde{h}_{i}^{k+1},\gamma_{i})-\mathbb{E}\bigl(\phi(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},\tilde{h}_{i}^{k+1},\gamma_{i})\!\bigm|\!\mathscr{S}_{k}\bigr)\bigr\}\overset{c}{\to}0.

(172)

To this end, for each
u\in\mathbb{R}
and
v\equiv(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k},y)\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
, define
\phi_{u,v},\bar{\phi}_{u,v}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\phi_{u,v}(z):=u\phi\bigl(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k},\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\bar{\alpha}_{\ell}^{k}\,x_{\ell}+\overset{\raisebox{-0.7pt}{\scalebox{0.7}[0.35]{$\perp$}}}{\tau}_{k+1}z,y\bigr)
and
\bar{\phi}_{u,v}(z):=\phi_{u,v}(z)-\mathbb{E}\bigl(\phi_{u,v}(Z)\bigr)
, where
Z\sim N(0,1)
. Since
\phi\in\mathrm{PL}_{k+2}(1)
, we deduce from Lemma
7.23
(a) that there exists
K^{\prime}>0
, depending only on the deterministic
\bar{\alpha}^{k}\equiv(\bar{\alpha}_{1}^{k},\dotsc,\bar{\alpha}_{k}^{k})
,
\overset{\raisebox{-0.7pt}{\scalebox{0.7}[0.35]{$\perp$}}}{\tau}_{k+1}
and
r
, such that
\bar{\phi}_{u,v}\in\mathrm{PL}_{1}(1,LK^{\prime}\lvert u\rvert)
for each
u\in\mathbb{R}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
. Now define the
\mathscr{S}_{k}
-measurable vectors
\upsilon_{k}^{(i)}:=(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},\gamma_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq n
, as in (
167
), and let
\tilde{E}_{r}
be as in (
156
). Then by Lemma
7.7
and (
201
) in Remark
7.13
, it follows as in (
156
), (
166
) and (
167
) that for each
n>k
and
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\lvert T_{n1}^{\prime}\rvert>\varepsilon\,|\,\mathscr{S}_{k})=\mathbb{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\phi}_{m_{i}^{0},\,\upsilon_{k}^{(i)}}(\tilde{Z}_{i}^{k+1})\biggr|\geq\varepsilon\Bigm|\mathscr{S}_{k}\biggr)\leq\tilde{E}_{r}(n,\varepsilon,LK^{\prime}\|m^{0}\|_{n}).

(173)

Now
m^{0}\equiv m^{0}(n)
is measurable with respect to
\mathscr{S}_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{S}_{k}\equiv\mathscr{S}_{k}(n)
for each
n
, and
\|m^{0}\|_{n}=O_{c}(1)
by
(A2)
, so we conclude as in (
158
) and (
159
) that
\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(\lvert T_{n1}^{\prime}\rvert>\varepsilon)<\infty
for all
\varepsilon>0
. Thus,
T_{n1}^{\prime}\overset{c}{\to}0
by Proposition
1.2
, as claimed in (
172
).

Finally, we prove that
T_{n2}\overset{c}{\to}0
. Let
L>0
be such that
\phi\in\mathrm{PL}_{k+2}(1,L)
, and for
n>k
and
1\leq i\leq n
, define
\upsilon_{k+1}^{(i)}=(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},h_{i}^{k+1,k},\gamma_{i})
and
\tilde{\upsilon}_{k+1}^{(i)}=(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},\tilde{h}_{i}^{k+1},\gamma_{i})=(h_{i}^{1},\dotsc,h_{i}^{k},h_{i}^{k+1,k}-\Delta_{i}^{k+1},\gamma_{i})
as in (
169
), where the final equality is obtained from (
144
). As in the proof of
\mathcal{H}_{1}(d)
, we now apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the fact that
\|{\cdot}\|_{n}\equiv\|{\cdot}\|_{n,2}\leq\|{\cdot}\|_{n,r}
to see that

\lvert T_{n2}\rvert\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\lvert m_{i}^{0}\rvert\bigl|\phi(\upsilon_{k+1}^{(i)})-\phi(\tilde{\upsilon}_{k+1}^{(i)})\bigr|\leq\frac{L}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\lvert m_{i}^{0}\rvert\lvert\Delta_{i}^{k+1}\rvert\leq L\|m^{0}\|_{n}\|\Delta^{k+1}\|_{n}.

Since
\|m^{0}\|_{n}\overset{c}{\to}\tau_{1}
by
(A2)
and
\|\Delta^{k+1}\|_{n}\leq\|\Delta^{k+1}\|_{n,r}=o_{c}(1)
by
\mathcal{H}_{k+1}(a)
, we conclude that
T_{n2}=o_{c}(1)
, as required. Together with (
170
), (
171
), (
172
), this yields
\mathcal{H}_{k+1}(d)
, and hence completes the inductive step for Proposition
6.16
.
∎

Proof of Remark
6.17
.

Under
(A0)
,
(A4)
and
(A5)
, if instead
(A1)
–
(A3)
hold with
\overset{c}{\to}
and
O_{c}(1)
replaced with
\overset{p}{\to}
and
O_{p}(1)
respectively, then as explained in the third bullet point in Remark
6.1
, we can make the same replacements in the proof of Proposition
6.16
and most of the arguments go through as before. However, a few alterations are required in the proofs of (c, d) and (g), which we now describe.

First, in the proof of
\mathcal{H}_{1}(d)
, the goal in (
155
) is now to show that
T_{n1}^{\prime}\overset{p}{\to}0
as
n\to\infty
. Instead of proceeding as in (
158
) and (
159
), we return to (
157
), where we note that if
\varepsilon>0
is fixed and
(A2)
takes the form
\|m^{0}\|_{n}\overset{p}{\to}\tau_{1}
, then
\mathbb{P}(\lvert T_{n1}^{\prime}\rvert>\varepsilon\,|\,\mathscr{S}_{0})\leq\tilde{E}_{r}(n,\varepsilon,L\tau_{1}\|m^{0}\|_{n})=o_{p}(1)
by Slutsky’s lemma and the definition of
\tilde{E}_{r}
in (
156
). Then for every
\varepsilon>0
, it follows from the bounded convergence theorem that
\mathbb{P}(\lvert T_{n1}^{\prime}\rvert>\varepsilon)=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathbb{P}(\lvert T_{n1}^{\prime}\rvert>\varepsilon\,|\,\mathscr{S}_{0})\bigr)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, so
T_{n1}^{\prime}\overset{p}{\to}0
, as desired.

In the proofs of
\mathcal{H}_{k+1}(c,d)
, the analogues of (
164
) and (
172
) can be derived from (
167
,
168
) and (
173
) respectively in much the same way; for the former, since
\|h^{\ell}\|_{n,r}=O_{p}(1)
for
1\leq\ell\leq k
by the modified
\mathcal{H}_{k}(b)
, (
168
) implies that
\breve{L}_{k}(n)\lesssim_{k,r}LK\bigl(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\,\|h^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r}\bigr)^{1/r^{\prime}}=O_{p}(1)
.

In addition,
\mathcal{H}_{k}(g)
now reads
b_{k}=\langle f_{k}^{\prime}(h^{k},\gamma)\rangle_{n}\overset{p}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(f_{k}^{\prime}(\bar{G}_{k},\bar{\gamma})\bigr)=\bar{b}_{k}
. To prove this, we can argue along subsequences, similarly to the proof of Corollary
7.21
(b).
∎

Proofs of Theorems
2.1
and
2.3
.

Theorem
2.1
follows from Theorem
2.3
, which in turn is a immediate consequence of Proposition
6.16
(c) and Corollary
7.21
(b).
∎

Proofs for Remark
6.1
.

(a)
Convergence in probability
: This is immediate from Remark
6.17
and Corollary
7.21
(b).

(b)
Almost sure convergence
: The random sequences
\Upsilon:=\bigl(m^{0}(n):n\in\mathbb{N}\bigr)
and
\Gamma:=\bigl(\gamma(n):n\in\mathbb{N}\bigr)
take values in
E:=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, whose cylindrical and Borel
\sigma
-algebras
coincide by
Kallenberg 1997
. Let
E^{*}
be the set of all
(u,v)\in E\times E
such that
(A1)
–
(A3)
hold when
\Upsilon=u\equiv\bigl(u(n):n\in\mathbb{N}\bigr)
and
\Gamma=v\equiv\bigl(v(n):n\in\mathbb{N}\bigr)
are non-random. It can be verified that
E^{*}
is a Borel subset of
E\times E
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\bar{\mu}^{k}:=N(0,\tau_{k}^{2})\otimes\pi
and
\mu_{n}^{k}:=\nu_{n}(h^{k},\gamma)
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In the special case where
(\Upsilon,\Gamma)\in E^{*}
is deterministic,
Theorem
2.1
implies that for each
k
, the resulting sequence of AMP iterates
\bigl(h^{k}(n):n\in\mathbb{N}\bigr)
satisfies
d_{r}(\mu_{n}^{k},\bar{\mu}^{k})\overset{c}{\to}0
. Note that for each
n
, we can write
d_{r}(\mu_{n}^{k},\bar{\mu}^{k})=d_{r}\bigl(\nu_{n}(h^{k},\gamma),\bar{\mu}^{k}\bigr)=g_{n}\bigl(m^{0}(n),\gamma(n),W(n)\bigr)
for some (non-random) Borel measurable
g_{n}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
. Indeed, we see from (
5
) that
h^{k}\equiv h^{k}(n)
is a deterministic Borel measurable function of
m^{0}(n)
,
\gamma(n)
and
W(n)
. Moreover for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the corresponding empirical distributions
\nu_{n}(x),\nu_{n}(x^{\prime})
of their components, we have

\bigl|d_{r}\bigl(\nu_{n}(x),\bar{\mu}^{k}\bigr)-d_{r}\bigl(\nu_{n}(x^{\prime}),\bar{\mu}^{k}\bigr)\bigr|\leq d_{r}\bigl(\nu_{n}(x),\nu_{n}(x^{\prime})\bigr)\leq\bigl(n^{-1}\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\lvert x_{i}-x_{i}^{\prime}\rvert^{r}\bigr)^{1/r}=\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{n,r}

since
d_{r}
is a metric, so
x\mapsto d_{r}\bigl(\nu_{n}(x),\bar{\mu}^{k}\bigr)
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Since
\{(a_{1},a_{2},\dotsc)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}:\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}=0\}
is a Borel subset of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, we conclude that
g\colon(u,v)\mapsto\mathbb{P}\bigl\{\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{n}\bigl(u(n),v(n),W(n)\bigr)=0\bigr\}

is a well-defined Borel measurable function on
E\times E
satisfying
g(u,v)=1
for all
(u,v)\in E^{*}
.

Now suppose more generally that
(\Upsilon,\Gamma)
and
\bigl(W(n):n\in\mathbb{N}\bigr)
are independent. If
(\Upsilon,\Gamma)\in E^{*}
almost surely, then for the corresponding sequence of AMP iterates
\bigl(h^{k}(n):n\in\mathbb{N}\bigr)
from (
5
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{r}(\mu_{n}^{k},\bar{\mu}^{k})=0\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{n}\bigl(m^{0}(n),\gamma(n),W(n)\bigr)=0\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl\{\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{n}\bigl(m^{0}(n),\gamma(n),W(n)\bigr)=0\Bigm|\Upsilon,\Gamma\Bigr)\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl(g(\Upsilon,\Gamma)\bigr)\geq\mathbb{E}\bigl(g(\Upsilon,\Gamma)\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\Upsilon,\Gamma)\in E^{*}\}}\bigr)=1,

where the third equality follows from the independence assumption and Lemma
7.7
. Therefore,
d_{r}(\mu_{n}^{k},\bar{\mu}^{k})\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
as
n\to\infty
, and moreover
\widetilde{d}_{r}(\mu_{n}^{k},\bar{\mu}^{k})\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
by Corollary
7.21
(a).
∎

6.6
Auxiliary results and proofs for Section
2

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

We proceed by induction on
k
, noting first that the base case
k=1
is trivial since
\bar{\mathrm{T}}^{[1]}=\tau_{1}^{2}>0
by
(A4)
. Now for
k\geq 2
and
a\equiv(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, recall the expression (
11
) for
a^{\top}\bar{\mathrm{T}}^{[k]}a
. If
a_{1}\neq 0=a_{2}=\dotsb=a_{k}
, then
a^{\top}\bar{\mathrm{T}}^{[k]}a=(a_{1}\tau_{1})^{2}>0
as in the base case.
On the other hand, if
a_{L}\neq 0
for some
2\leq L\leq k
, then by
(A4)
, we can find

B_{L}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
with
\pi(B_{L})>0
such that
x_{L-1}\mapsto a_{L}f_{L-1}(x_{L-1},y)
is non-constant whenever
y\in B_{L}
. For all such
y
, note that
(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k-1})\mapsto\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}a_{\ell}f_{\ell-1}(x_{\ell-1},y)
is non-constant on
\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
. Now by the inductive hypothesis,
(G_{1},\dotsc,G_{k-1})
has a positive definite covariance matrix
\bar{\mathrm{T}}^{[k-1]}
, so the random variable
a_{1}F_{0}(y)+\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}a_{\ell}f_{\ell-1}(G_{\ell-1},y)
is non-degenerate whenever
y\in B_{L}
. Since
\bar{\gamma}\sim\pi
is independent of
G_{1},\dotsc,G_{k-1}
and
\mathbb{P}(\bar{\gamma}\in B_{L})=\pi(B_{L})>0
, it follows that
a_{1}F_{0}(\bar{\gamma})+\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}a_{\ell}f_{\ell-1}(G_{\ell-1},\bar{\gamma})
is also non-degenerate. Thus, in all cases, it follows from (
11
) and
(A3)
that
a^{\top}\bar{\mathrm{T}}^{[k]}a>0
whenever
a\neq 0
, as claimed.
∎

Proof of Remark
6.5
.

Since
\tilde{f}_{0}
is Lipschitz and
\bar{\eta},\bar{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)
, we have
\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl\|\bigl(\tilde{f}_{0}(\bar{\eta},\bar{\gamma}),\bar{\gamma}\bigr)\bigr\|^{2}\bigr\}<\infty
, so
\mu^{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(2)
. By Corollary
7.21
(b), an equivalent formulation of
(A1+)
is that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(m_{i}^{0},\gamma_{i})\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(\tilde{f}_{0}(\bar{\eta},\bar{\gamma}),\bar{\gamma}\bigr)\bigr\}

for all
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{2}(2)
. In particular,
(x,y)\mapsto x^{2}
lies in
\mathrm{PL}_{2}(2)
, so
\|m^{0}\|_{n}^{2}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\lvert m_{i}^{0}\rvert^{2}\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(\tilde{f}_{0}(\bar{\eta},\bar{\gamma})^{2}\bigr)=:\tau_{1}^{2}
, which yields the first part of
(A2)
. Moreover, the function
F_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
F_{0}(y):=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\tilde{f}_{0}(\bar{\eta},y)\bigr)
is Lipschitz, and since
\bar{\eta},\bar{\gamma}
are independent, it follows from Lemma
7.7
and Jensen’s inequality that

\mathbb{E}\bigl(F_{0}(\bar{\gamma})^{2}\bigr)=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\bigl(\tilde{f}_{0}(\bar{\eta},\bar{\gamma})\!\bigm|\!\bar{\gamma}\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\bigl(\tilde{f}_{0}(\bar{\eta},\bar{\gamma})^{2}\!\bigm|\!\bar{\gamma}\bigr)\bigr\}=\tau_{1}^{2}.

For each Lipschitz
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, Lemma
7.22
ensures that
(x,y)\mapsto x\phi(y)
belongs to
\mathrm{PL}_{2}(2)
, so

\langle m^{0},\phi(\gamma)\rangle_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}^{0}\,\phi(\gamma_{i})\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(\tilde{f}_{0}(\bar{\eta},\bar{\gamma})\cdot\phi(\bar{\gamma})\bigr)=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\bigl(\tilde{f}_{0}(\bar{\eta},\bar{\gamma})\!\bigm|\!\bar{\gamma}\bigr)\phi(\bar{\gamma})\bigr\}=\mathbb{E}\bigl(F_{0}(\bar{\gamma})\phi(\bar{\gamma})\bigr),

where the final equality again follows from Lemma
7.7
. Therefore,
(A3)
also holds.
∎

The following auxiliary result is used in the proof of Proposition
6.16
(a) to control the third summand in the deviation term
\Delta^{k+1}
defined in (
144
).

Lemma 6.18
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,1\dotsc,n-1\}
, let
\tilde{Z}^{k+1}\equiv\tilde{Z}^{k+1}(n)
be as in Proposition
6.11
, so that
\tilde{Z}^{k+1}\sim N_{n}(0,I_{n})
is independent of
\mathscr{S}_{k}
. If
\xi_{1},\dotsc,\xi_{k}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(0,1)
and
r\geq 1
, then
\|P_{k}\tilde{Z}^{k+1}\|_{n,r}
is stochastically dominated by
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,\lvert\xi_{i}\rvert/n^{1/(r\vee 2)}
.

Proof.

Note that
P_{k}
is an
\mathscr{S}_{k}
-measurable
projection matrix of rank
r_{k}\leq k
. Since
\tilde{Z}^{k+1}
is independent of
\mathscr{S}_{k}
, it therefore has conditional distribution
N_{n}(0,I_{n})
given
\mathscr{S}_{k}
by Remark
7.4
. Now let
\xi_{1},\dotsc,\xi_{k}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(0,1)
be independent of
\mathscr{S}_{k}
and let
\{\tilde{m}^{1},\dotsc,\tilde{m}^{r_{k}}\}
be any
\mathscr{S}_{k}
-measurable orthonormal basis of
\Img(M_{k})=\Img(P_{k})
, as in Remark
6.10
. Recall that if
Z\sim N_{n}(0,I_{n})
and
P\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a deterministic projection matrix of rank
p
, then
PZ\sim N(0,P)
, which is also the distribution of
\sum_{i=1}^{p}\zeta_{i}u_{i}
when
\zeta_{1},\dotsc,\zeta_{p}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(0,1)
and
\{u_{1},\dotsc,u_{p}\}
is any orthonormal basis of
\Img(P)
. We deduce from this and Lemma
7.6
(b) that
P_{k}\tilde{Z}^{k+1}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{r_{k}}\xi_{i}\tilde{m}^{i}
both have conditional distribution
N_{n}(0,P_{k})
given
\mathscr{S}_{k}
. This implies that
\|P_{k}\tilde{Z}^{k+1}\|_{n,r}\overset{d}{=}\|\sum_{i=1}^{r_{k}}\xi_{i}\tilde{m}^{i}\|_{n,r}
.

Now for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have
\|x\|_{n,r}=n^{-1/r}\|x\|_{r}\leq n^{-1/(r\vee 2)}\|x\|_{2}
by Hölder’s inequality and the fact that
\|{\cdot}\|_{p^{\prime}}\leq\|{\cdot}\|_{p}
for
1\leq p\leq p^{\prime}
. Since
\|\tilde{m}^{i}\|_{2}=1
for all
i
by definition, it follows from this and the triangle inequality for
\|{\cdot}\|_{n,r}
that
\|\sum_{i=1}^{r_{k}}\xi_{i}\tilde{m}^{i}\|_{n,r}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r_{k}}\,\lvert\xi_{i}\rvert\,\|\tilde{m}^{i}\|_{n,r}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r_{k}}\,\lvert\xi_{i}\rvert/n^{1/(r\vee 2)}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,\lvert\xi_{i}\rvert/n^{1/(r\vee 2)}
. Combining this with the conclusion of the previous paragraph yields the result.
∎

6.7
AMP with matrix-valued iterates

As mentioned in Section
2.1
, state evolution characterisations can be obtained for more general abstract AMP recursions in which the iterates are matrices rather than vectors. Here, we will briefly describe the extended version of the asymmetric iteration (
13
), which is used to establish the master theorem for GAMP in Section
4.1
.

For
n,p\in\mathbb{N}
, let
W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be as in
(B0)
. For
\ell_{E},\ell_{H}\in\mathbb{N}
, let
(g_{k},f_{k+1}:k\in\mathbb{N}_{0})
be two sequences of Lipschitz functions
g_{k}\colon\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{E}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{H}}
and
f_{k+1}\colon\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{H}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{E}}
, which are applied row-wise to matrices. Given
Q^{-1}:=0\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ell_{H}}
,
B_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{E}\times\ell_{H}}
and
M^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times\ell_{E}}
, inductively define

\displaystyle E^{k}

\displaystyle:=WM^{k}-Q^{k-1}B_{k}^{\top},\quad\;\;

\displaystyle Q^{k}

\displaystyle:=g_{k}(E^{k},\gamma),\quad\;\;

\displaystyle C_{k}

\displaystyle:=n^{-1}\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{k}^{\prime}(E_{i}^{k},\gamma_{i}),

(174)

\displaystyle H^{k+1}

\displaystyle:=W^{\top}Q^{k}-M^{k}\,C_{k}^{\top},\quad\;\;

\displaystyle M^{k+1}

\displaystyle:=f_{k+1}(H^{k+1},\beta),\quad\;\;

\displaystyle B_{k+1}

\displaystyle:=n^{-1}\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{p}f_{k+1}^{\prime}(H_{j}^{k+1},\beta_{j})

for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Here,
E_{i}^{k}
and
H_{j}^{k+1}
denote the
i^{th}
and
j^{th}
rows of
E^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ell_{E}}
and
H^{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times\ell_{H}}
respectively, and
g_{k}^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{E}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{H}\times\ell_{E}}
and
f_{k+1}^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{H}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{E}\times\ell_{H}}
are bounded, Borel measurable functions that agree with the derivatives (Jacobians) of
g_{k},f_{k+1}
respectively with respect to their first arguments, wherever the latter are defined.

Consider now a sequence of recursions (
174
) indexed by
n
and
p\equiv p_{n}
with
n/p\to\delta\in(0,\infty)
as
n\to\infty
, and assume appropriate analogues of
(B0)
–
(B5)
with
r\in[2,\infty)
. In particular, suppose in place of
(B2)
that
(M^{0})^{\top}M^{0}/n\overset{c}{\to}\Sigma_{0}
for some non-negative definite
\Sigma_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{E}\times\ell_{E}}
, and that
p^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{E}}\,\lvert M_{ij}^{0}\rvert^{r}=O_{c}(1)
.
The state evolution recursion for (
174
) is then defined analogously to that in (
14
), via

\begin{split}\mathrm{T}_{k+1}&:=\mathbb{E}\bigl(g_{k}(G_{k}^{\sigma},\bar{\gamma})^{\top}g_{k}(G_{k}^{\sigma},\bar{\gamma})\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{H}\times\ell_{H}},\\
\Sigma_{k+1}&:=\delta^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(f_{k+1}(G_{k+1}^{\tau},\bar{\beta})^{\top}f_{k+1}(G_{k+1}^{\tau},\bar{\beta})\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{E}\times\ell_{E}}\end{split}

(175)

for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, where we take
G_{k}^{\sigma}\sim N_{\ell_{E}}(0,\Sigma_{k})
to be independent of
\bar{\beta}\sim\pi_{\bar{\beta}}
, and
G_{k+1}^{\tau}\sim N_{\ell_{H}}(0,\mathrm{T}_{k+1})
to be independent of
\bar{\gamma}\sim\pi_{\bar{\gamma}}
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, it can be shown that the empirical distributions of the rows of
(E^{k}\;\gamma)
and
(H^{k+1}\;\beta)
converge completely in
d_{r}
to
N_{\ell_{E}}(0,\Sigma_{k})\otimes\pi_{\gamma}
and
N_{\ell_{H}}(0,\mathrm{T}_{k+1})\otimes\pi_{\beta}
respectively as
n,p\to\infty
with
n/p\to\delta
. Similarly as in Remark
2.4
, these limiting distributions remain unchanged if one or both of
C_{k},B_{k+1}
are replaced with the deterministic matrices
\bar{C}_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\bigl(g_{k}^{\prime}(G_{k}^{\Sigma},\bar{\gamma})\bigr)
and
\bar{B}_{k+1}:=\mathbb{E}\bigl(f_{k+1}^{\prime}(G_{k+1}^{\mathrm{T}},\bar{\beta})\bigr)
respectively. Moreover, by generalising the definitions (
15
)–(
16
) of the limiting covariance matrices in line with (
175
), one can obtain the
d_{r}
limits of the joint empirical distributions for (
174
) above.

The proofs of these results are conceptually very similar to that of Theorem
2.5
. For further details, see
Javanmard and Montanari 2013
, who first consider a generalisation of the symmetric iteration (
5
) with matrix-valued iterates, and then handle the asymmetric case by a reduction argument.

6.8
Proofs for Section
3

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

As described in the proof sketch on page 0, we introduce the recursion (
32
) given by
u^{1}\equiv u^{1}(n)=W\hat{v}^{0}=Wg_{0}(v^{0})
and

\displaystyle u^{k+1}\equiv u^{k+1}(n)

\displaystyle=Wg_{k}(u^{k}+\mu_{k}v)-\tilde{b}_{k}g_{k-1}(u^{k-1}+\mu_{k-1}v)

\displaystyle=Wf_{k}(u^{k},v)-\tilde{b}_{k}f_{k-1}(u^{k-1},v)

for
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
f_{k}(x,y)=g_{k}(x+\mu_{k}y)
and
f_{k}^{\prime}(x,y)=g_{k}^{\prime}(x+\mu_{k}y)
for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\tilde{b}_{k}\equiv\tilde{b}_{k}(n)=\langle g_{k}^{\prime}(u^{k}+\mu_{k}v)\rangle_{n}=\langle f_{k}^{\prime}(u^{k},v)\rangle_{n}
. First, we verify that this is an iteration of the form (
5
) to which we can apply the master theorems from Section
2.1
for symmetric AMP. Indeed, it follows from
(M0)
and
(M1)
respectively that (
32
) satisfies
(A0)
and
(A1+)
, where the latter holds with
m^{0}=v
,
\gamma=v
,
\bar{\gamma}=V\sim\pi
,
\bar{\eta}=U
and
\tilde{f}_{0}(x,y)=f_{0}(\mu_{0}x+\sigma_{0}y)
for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. By Remark
6.5
,
(A1+)
implies that
(A1)
–
(A3)
hold with
r=2
and
\tau_{1}=\clim_{n\to\infty}\|\hat{v}^{0}\|_{n}^{2}=\sigma_{1}^{2}
. As verified in (
33
), the state evolution parameters
(\tau_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N})
for (
32
) satisfy
\tau_{k}^{2}=\sigma_{k}^{2}
for all
k
in view of (
25
). Finally, by
(M2)
, each
f_{k}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz and the corresponding
f_{k}^{\prime}
satisfies
(A5)
.

Consequently, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows from Theorem
2.3
that

\sup_{\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{k+1}(2,1)}\;\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(v_{i}^{0},v_{i}^{1},\dotsc,v_{i}^{k},v_{i})-\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi(\mu_{0}V+\sigma_{0}U,\sigma_{1}G_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{k}G_{k},V)\bigr)\biggr|\overset{c}{\to}0

as
n\to\infty
, where
(\sigma_{1}G_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{k}G_{k})\sim N_{k}(0,\bar{\Sigma}^{[k]})
is taken to be independent of
(U,V)
from
(M1)
. Since
\Phi_{k}\colon(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k},y)\mapsto(x_{1}+\mu_{1}y,\dotsc,x_{k}+\mu_{k}y,y)
is a linear map with Lipschitz constant
\tilde{L}_{k}:=\|(\mu_{1},\dotsc,\mu_{k},1)\|
, we have
\tilde{L}_{k}^{-2}(\psi\circ\Phi_{k})\in\mathrm{PL}_{k+1}(2,1)
whenever
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{k+1}(2,1)
,
so it follows from the display above that

\displaystyle\sup_{\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{k+2}(2,1)}\;\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(v_{i}^{0},v_{i}^{1}+\mu_{1}v_{i},\dotsc,v_{i}^{k}+\mu_{k}v_{i},v_{i})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi(\mu_{0}V+\sigma_{0}U,\mu_{1}V+\sigma_{1}G_{1},\dotsc,\mu_{k}V+\sigma_{k}G_{k},V)\bigr)\biggr|\overset{c}{\to}0

(176)

as
n\to\infty
. Defining
\tilde{\Delta}^{k}\equiv\tilde{\Delta}^{k}(n):=v^{k}-(u^{k}+\mu_{k}v)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can apply Lemma
7.24
to see that

\displaystyle\sup_{\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{k+2}(2,1)}\;\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(v_{i}^{0},v_{i}^{1},\dotsc,v_{i}^{k},v_{i})-\psi(v_{i}^{0},v_{i}^{1}+\mu_{1}v_{i},\dotsc,v_{i}^{k}+\mu_{k}v_{i},v_{i})\biggr|

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\leq\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\|\tilde{\Delta}^{\ell}\|_{n}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\biggl(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\,\bigl(\|v^{\ell}\|_{n}+\|u^{\ell}+\mu_{\ell}v\|_{n}\bigr)+2\bigl(\|v^{0}\|_{n}+\|v\|_{n}\bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\leq\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\|\tilde{\Delta}^{\ell}\|_{n}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\biggl(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\,\bigl(\|\tilde{\Delta}^{\ell}\|_{n}+2\|u^{\ell}+\mu_{\ell}v\|_{n}\bigr)+2\bigl(\|v^{0}\|_{n}+\|v\|_{n}\bigr)\biggr)

(177)

for all
k
and
n
, where
\|{\cdot}\|_{n}\equiv\|{\cdot}\|_{n,2}=n^{-1/2}\,\|{\cdot}\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows from
(M1)
and (
176
) that

\displaystyle\|v^{0}\|_{n}\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl((\mu_{0}V+\sigma_{0}U)^{2}\bigr)=\mu_{0}^{2}+\sigma_{0}^{2},\qquad\|v\|_{n}^{2}\to\mathbb{E}(V^{2})=1

and

\displaystyle\|u^{\ell}+\mu_{\ell}v\|_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,(u_{i}^{\ell}+\mu_{\ell}v_{i})^{2}\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl((\mu_{\ell}V+\sigma_{\ell}G_{\ell})^{2}\bigr)=\mu_{\ell}^{2}+\sigma_{\ell}^{2}

(178)

as
n\to\infty
. We will now establish by induction on
k\in\mathbb{N}
that

\|\tilde{\Delta}^{k}\|_{n}=\|v^{k}-(u^{k}+\mu_{k}v)\|_{n}\overset{c}{\to}0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty

(179)

and hence that the conclusion (
27
) of Theorem
3.1
holds for every
k
. For the base case
k=1
, we have
\|v\|_{n}\overset{c}{\to}1
by (
23
) and
\lambda\langle\hat{v}^{0},v\rangle_{n}\overset{c}{\to}\mu_{1}
by (
24
), so

\|\tilde{\Delta}^{1}\|_{n}=\|v^{1}-(u^{1}+\mu_{1}v)\|_{n}=\|A\hat{v}^{0}-(W\hat{v}^{0}+\mu_{1}v)\|_{n}=\lvert\lambda\langle\hat{v}^{0},v\rangle_{n}-\mu_{1}\rvert\,\|v\|_{n}\overset{c}{\to}0

as
n\to\infty
. It follows from this and (
176
)–(
178
) that (
27
) holds when
k=1
. For a general
k\geq 2
, we write

\displaystyle\tilde{\Delta}^{k+1}\equiv\tilde{\Delta}^{k+1}(n)

\displaystyle=v^{k+1}-(u^{k+1}+\mu_{k+1}v)

\displaystyle=Ag_{k}(v^{k})-b_{k}g_{k-1}(v^{k-1})-\bigl(Wg_{k}(u^{k}+\mu_{k}v)-\tilde{b}_{k}g_{k-1}(u^{k-1}+\mu_{k-1}v)+\mu_{k+1}v\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\lambda\langle v,g_{k}(v^{k})\rangle_{n}-\mu_{k+1}\bigr)v+W\bigl(g_{k}(v^{k})-g_{k}(u^{k}+\mu_{k}v)\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 127.46822pt+\bigl(\tilde{b}_{k}g_{k-1}(u^{k-1}+\mu_{k-1}v)-b_{k}g_{k-1}(v^{k-1})\bigr)

\displaystyle=:R_{n1}+R_{n2}+R_{n3}

(180)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and consider
R_{n1},R_{n2},R_{n3}
in turn. First, since
(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k},y)\mapsto yg_{k}(x_{k})
belongs to
\mathrm{PL}_{k+1}(2)
in view of Lemma
7.22
, it follows from the inductive hypothesis (
27
) and the definition of
\mu_{k+1}
in (
25
) that

\lambda\langle v,g_{k}(v^{k})\rangle_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda v_{i}\,g_{k}(v_{i}^{k})\overset{c}{\to}\lambda\mathbb{E}\bigl(Vg_{k}(\mu_{k}V+\sigma_{k}G_{k})\bigr)=\mu_{k+1}
. Together with (
178
), this implies that
\|R_{n1}\|_{n}=\lvert\lambda\langle v,g_{k}(v^{k})\rangle_{n}-\mu_{k+1}\rvert\,\|v\|_{n}\overset{c}{\to}0
as
n\to\infty
.

Next, since
\|W\|\equiv\|W\|_{2\to 2}=O_{c}(1)

(
Anderson et al. 2010
;
Knowles and Yin 2013
, e.g.)
and
g_{k}
is
L_{k}
-Lipschitz for some
L_{k}>0
, the inductive hypothesis (
179
) ensures that

\|R_{n2}\|_{n}\leq\|W\|\,\|g_{k}(v^{k})-g_{k}(u^{k}+\mu_{k}v)\|_{n}\leq L_{k}\|W\|\,\|v^{k}-(u^{k}+\mu_{k}v)\|_{n}=L_{k}\|W\|\,\|\tilde{\Delta}^{k}\|_{n}\overset{c}{\to}0

as
n\to\infty
. Similarly,
\|\tilde{\Delta}^{k-1}\|_{n}\overset{c}{\to}0
by induction and
g_{k-1}
is
L_{k-1}
-Lipschitz for some
L_{k-1}>0
, so as a first step towards controlling
\|R_{n3}\|_{n}
, we have

\|g_{k-1}(u^{k-1}+\mu_{k-1}v)-g_{k-1}(v^{k-1})\|_{n}\leq L_{k-1}\|\tilde{\Delta}^{k-1}\|_{n}\overset{c}{\to}0.

Note also that since
(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k},y)\mapsto g_{k-1}(x_{k-1})^{2}
lies in
\mathrm{PL}_{k+1}(2)
by Lemma
7.22
, it follows from (
176
) that

\|g_{k-1}(u^{k-1}+\mu_{k-1}v)\|_{n}^{2}\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(g_{k-1}(\mu_{k-1}V+\sigma_{k-1}G_{k-1})^{2}\bigr)=\sigma_{k}^{2}

as
n\to\infty
. Furthermore, since
g_{k}^{\prime}
satisfies
(M2)
, we can apply the inductive hypothesis (
27
) and argue as in the proof of Proposition
6.16
(f) to see that

b_{k}\equiv b_{k}(n)=\langle g_{k}^{\prime}(v^{k})\rangle_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{k}^{\prime}(v_{i}^{k})\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(g_{k}^{\prime}(\mu_{k}V+\sigma_{k}G_{k})\bigr)
. Similar reasoning based on (
176
) yields
\tilde{b}_{k}\equiv\tilde{b}_{k}(n)=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{k}^{\prime}(v_{i}^{k}+\mu_{k}v_{i})\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(g_{k}^{\prime}(\mu_{k}V+\sigma_{k}G_{k})\bigr)
, so
\tilde{b}_{k}(n)-b_{k}(n)\overset{c}{\to}0
as
n\to\infty
.
Putting everything together, we conclude that

\|R_{n3}\|_{n}\leq\lvert\tilde{b}_{k}-b_{k}\rvert\|g_{k-1}(u^{k-1}+\mu_{k-1}v)\|_{n}+\lvert b_{k}\rvert\|g_{k-1}(u^{k-1}+\mu_{k-1}v)-g_{k-1}(v^{k-1})\|_{n}\overset{c}{\to}0,

and hence that
\|\tilde{\Delta}^{k+1}\|_{n}\leq\|R_{n1}\|_{n}+\|R_{n2}\|_{n}+\|R_{n3}\|_{n}\overset{c}{\to}0
as
n\to\infty
. Combining this with (
176
)–(
178
) yields the desired conclusion (
27
), so the inductive step is complete.
∎

Proof of Corollary
3.2
.

For
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{2}(2)
, note that since
g_{k}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz by assumption,
(x_{0},x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k},y)\mapsto\psi\bigl(g_{k}(x_{k}),y\bigr)
is a
\mathrm{PL}_{k+2}(2)
function to which we can apply (
27
). This yields (
28
), which specialises to (
29
) when
\psi=\psi_{2}\colon(x,y)\mapsto(x-y)^{2}
is squared error loss. Finally, by considering the
\mathrm{PL}_{2}(2)
functions
(x,y)\mapsto yg_{k}(x)
,
(x,y)\mapsto g_{k}(x)^{2}
and
(x,y)\mapsto y^{2}
, we deduce from (
28
) that as
n\to\infty
, we have

\langle\hat{v}^{k},v\rangle_{n}\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(Vg_{k}(\mu_{k}V+\sigma_{k}G_{k})\bigr)=\mu_{k+1}

as in the paragraph after (
180
) above,

\|\hat{v}^{k}\|_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{k}(v_{i}^{k})^{2}\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl(g_{k}(\mu_{k}V+\sigma_{k}G_{k})^{2}\bigr)=\sigma_{k+1}^{2}

and
\|v\|_{n}^{2}\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}(V^{2})=1
as in (
23
). Combining these, we obtain (
30
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

Fix
\mu\neq 0
and
\sigma>0
, and let
V\sim\pi
and
G\sim N(0,1)
be independent. Then
\mu V+\sigma G
has Lebesgue density
y\mapsto p(y):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{\sigma}(y-\mu x)\,d\pi(x)>0
, where
\phi_{\sigma}\colon z\mapsto(\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma)^{-1}e^{-z^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}
is the density of
\sigma G\sim N(0,\sigma^{2})
. Moreover, since all the derivatives of
\phi_{\sigma}
are bounded on
\mathbb{R}
, we can differentiate repeatedly under the integral sign to see that
p^{(j)}(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{\sigma}^{(j)}(y-\mu x)\,d\pi(x)
for all
y
and
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, so
p
is a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}
. For each
y\in\mathbb{R}
, define
\pi_{y}
to be the distribution on
\mathbb{R}
with density (i.e. Radon–Nikodym derivative)

\frac{d\pi_{y}}{d\pi}\colon x\mapsto\frac{\phi_{\sigma}(y-\mu x)}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{\sigma}(y-\mu x^{\prime})\,d\pi(x^{\prime})}=\frac{\phi_{\sigma}(y-\mu x)}{p(y)}

(181)

with respect to
\pi
. It is easily verified that
\pi_{y}
is the “conditional distribution of
V
given
\mu V+\sigma G=y
”, formally in the sense of Remark
7.5
(II).
It follows from this and
Dudley 2002
that taking
V_{y}\sim\pi_{y}
and defining
g(y):=\mathbb{E}(V_{y})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x\,d\pi_{y}(x)
for
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\mathbb{E}(V\,|\,\mu V+\sigma G)=g(\mu V+\sigma G)
. For each
y
, note that

g(y)=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}}x\,\phi_{\sigma}(y-\mu x)\,d\pi(x)}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{\sigma}(y-\mu x)\,d\pi(x)}=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mu^{-1}\bigl(y\phi_{\sigma}(y-\mu x)+\phi_{\sigma}^{\prime}(y-\mu x)\bigr)\,d\pi(x)}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{\sigma}(y-\mu x)\,d\pi(x)}=\frac{y+\sigma^{2}(\log p)^{\prime}(y)}{\mu},

(182)

so (
35
) holds and
g
is infinitely differentiable on
\mathbb{R}
, and by similar calculations,

g^{\prime}(y)=\frac{1+\sigma^{2}(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(y)}{\mu}=\frac{\mu}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{\sigma^{2}\bigl(1+\sigma^{2}(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(y)\bigr)}{\mu^{2}}=\frac{\mu}{\sigma^{2}}\Var(V_{y})\geq 0.

We now consider in turn the two conditions on
\pi
in the statement of the lemma.

(i)

If
V\sim\pi
has a log-concave density, then the density
p
of
\mu V+\sigma G
is also log-concave
(
Prékopa 1980
)
, so
(\log p)^{\prime\prime}\leq 0
on
\mathbb{R}
. Thus,
0\leq g^{\prime}\leq\lvert\mu\rvert^{-1}
on
\mathbb{R}
, so
g
is
Lipschitz with constant
\lvert\mu\rvert^{-1}
.

(ii)

Suppose first that
\pi
is supported on a compact interval
[a,b]
. Then for each
y\in\mathbb{R}
, the distribution
\pi_{y}
has a density with respect to
\pi
(by definition), so it is also supported on
[a,b]
. Thus,
\Var(V_{y})\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(V_{y}-(a+b)/2\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}\leq(b-a)^{2}/4
for all
y
, whence
g
is Lipschitz with constant
\lvert\mu\rvert(b-a)^{2}/(4\sigma^{2})
, and

{-1}\leq\sigma^{2}(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(y)\leq\frac{\mu^{2}(b-a)^{2}}{4\sigma^{2}}-1.

(183)

More generally, suppose that
\pi
is the distribution of
U_{0}+V_{0}
, where
U_{0}\sim N(0,\sigma_{0}^{2})
with
\sigma_{0}\geq 0
, and
V_{0}\sim\pi_{0}
is independent of
U_{0}
and supported on some compact interval
[a,b]
. Then
p
is the density of
\mu V+\sigma G\overset{d}{=}\mu U_{0}+\sqrt{\sigma^{2}+\mu^{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}}\,G
, so it follows from (
182
) and (
183
) that

\frac{1}{\lvert\mu\rvert}\biggl(1-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\sigma^{2}+\mu^{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}}\biggr)\leq\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert\leq\frac{1}{\lvert\mu\rvert}\,\biggl\{1+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\sigma^{2}+\mu^{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}}\biggl(\frac{\mu^{2}(b-a)^{2}}{4(\sigma^{2}+\mu^{2}\sigma_{0}^{2})}-1\biggr)\biggr\}

on
\mathbb{R}
. The expression on the right hand side is therefore a Lipschitz constant for
g
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.7
.

Let
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,\infty)
be any measurable loss function, and fix
s_{1},s_{2}\in(0,\infty)
with
s_{1}>s_{2}
. Taking
G^{\prime}\sim N(0,s_{1}^{2}-s_{2}^{2})
,
V\sim\pi
and
G\sim N(0,1)
to be jointly independent, we first claim that

R_{\pi,\psi}(s_{2}^{-2})=\inf_{g}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(g(V+s_{2}G),V\bigr)\bigr\}=\inf_{\tilde{g}}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(\tilde{g}(V+s_{2}G,G^{\prime}),V\bigr)\bigr\},

(184)

where the infima are taken over all measurable
g\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\tilde{g}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
respectively. Indeed, the first equality holds since
V+s_{2}G=s_{2}(\sqrt{\rho}V+G)
when
\rho=s_{2}^{-2}
, and the middle expression is clearly bounded below by the final one, so it remains to prove the reverse inequality. For any fixed
\tilde{g}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\tilde{\Psi}(a):=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(\tilde{g}(V+s_{2}G,a),V\bigr)\bigr\}\geq\inf_{g}\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(g(V+s_{2}G),V\bigr)\bigr\}=R_{\pi,\psi}(s_{2}^{-2})

for all
a\in\mathbb{R}
, so it follows from Lemma
7.7
that
\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(\tilde{g}(V+s_{2}G,G^{\prime}),V\bigr)\bigr\}=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\tilde{\Psi}(G^{\prime})\bigr)\geq R_{\pi,\psi}(s_{2}^{-2})
, and hence that (
184
) holds. Since
(V,V+s_{2}G+G^{\prime})\overset{d}{=}(V,V+s_{1}G)
, we deduce that

\displaystyle R_{\pi,\psi}(s_{2}^{-2})=\inf_{\tilde{g}}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(\tilde{g}(V+s_{2}G,G^{\prime}),V\bigr)\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{g}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(g(V+s_{2}G+G^{\prime}),V\bigr)\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{g}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(g(V+s_{1}G),V\bigr)\bigr\}=R_{\pi,\psi}(s_{1}^{-2}).

In addition, arguing as above for (
184
), we have

R_{\pi,\psi}(s_{1}^{-2})=\inf_{g}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(g(V+s_{1}G),V\bigr)\bigr\}\leq\inf_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi(a,V)\bigr)=\inf_{g}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(g(G),V\bigr)\bigr\}=R_{\pi,\psi}(0).

Thus,
\rho\mapsto R_{\pi,\psi}(\rho)
is non-increasing on
[0,\infty)
.

Finally, fix
\rho\in(0,\infty)
and for each
y\in\mathbb{R}
, let
V_{y}\sim\pi_{y}
be a random variable whose density with respect to
\pi
is given by (
181
) with
\mu=\sqrt{\rho}
and
\sigma=1
, so that
\pi_{y}
is the conditional (i.e. posterior) distribution of
V
given
\sqrt{\rho}V+G=y
. It follows from
Brown and Purves 1973
that if the posterior risk function

r_{y}\colon a\mapsto\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi(a,V_{y})\bigr)

attains its infimum on
\mathbb{R}
for Lebesgue almost every
y\in\mathbb{R}
, then there exists a measurable
g^{*}\equiv g_{\rho}^{*}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with
g^{*}(y)\in\argmin_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi(a,V_{y})\bigr)
for Lebesgue almost every
y\in\mathbb{R}
, whence
R_{\pi,\psi}(\rho)=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(g^{*}(\sqrt{\rho}V+G),V\bigr)\bigr\}
. This is the case (for every
\rho
) if
\psi(x,y)=\Psi(x-y)
for some convex function
\Psi
with
\Psi(u)\to\infty
as
\lvert u\rvert\to\infty
, in which case
r_{y}\colon a\mapsto\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi(V_{y},a)\bigr)
is convex with
r_{y}(a)\to\infty
as
\lvert a\rvert\to\infty
, for each
y\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Corollary 6.19
.

Given independent random variables
V\sim\pi
and
G\sim N(0,1)
, the function
\rho\mapsto\mmse(\rho):=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(V-\mathbb{E}(V\,|\,\sqrt{\rho}V+G)\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}
is non-increasing on
[0,\infty)
. Moreover, if
V
satisfies one of the conditions of Lemma
3.8
, then
\rho\mapsto\mmse(\rho)
is also continuous on
(0,\infty)
.

Proof of Corollary
6.19
.

Recall that whenever
X,Y
are random variables with
\mathbb{E}(X^{2})<\infty
, it follows from an orthogonal decomposition of the type (
36
) that

\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(X-\mathbb{E}(X\,|\,Y)\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}=\min_{g}\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(X-g(Y)\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}
, where the minimum is over all measurable functions
g\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Thus, by Lemma
3.7
,
\rho\mapsto\mmse(\rho)
is non-increasing on
[0,\infty)
.

Now fix
s_{1}>s_{2}>0
, and as in the proof of Lemma
3.7
, let
G^{\prime}\sim N(0,s_{1}^{2}-s_{2}^{2})
,
V\sim\pi
and
G\sim N(0,1)
be jointly independent, so that
(V,V+s_{2}G+G^{\prime})\overset{d}{=}(V,V+s_{1}G)
. Then under the conditions of Lemma
3.8
, it follows from (i) and (ii) in its proof that there exists a Lipschitz
g_{2}^{*}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with
g_{2}^{*}(V+s_{2}G)=\mathbb{E}(V\,|\,V+s_{2}G)=\mathbb{E}(V\,|\,s_{2}^{-1}V+G)
and Lipschitz constant
L_{s_{2}}\leq C_{\pi}(1\vee s_{2}^{-2})
, where
C_{\pi}>0
depends only on
\pi
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mmse(s_{2}^{-2})\leq\mmse(s_{1}^{-2})

\displaystyle=\min_{g}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(V-g(V+s_{1}G)\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(V-g_{2}^{*}(V+s_{1}G)\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(V-g_{2}^{*}(V+s_{2}G+G^{\prime})\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(V-g_{2}^{*}(V+s_{2}G)\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}+\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(g_{2}^{*}(V+s_{2}G)-g_{2}^{*}(V+s_{2}G+G^{\prime})\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\mmse(s_{2}^{-2})+L_{s_{2}}\,\mathbb{E}(\lvert G^{\prime}\rvert^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\mmse(s_{2}^{-2})+C_{\pi}\,(s_{1}^{2}s_{2}^{2}\vee s_{1}^{2})(s_{2}^{-2}-s_{1}^{-2}).

(185)

To justify the equality in the third-last line, note that
g_{2}^{*}(V+s_{2}G)=\mathbb{E}(V\,|\,V+s_{2}G,G^{\prime})
by the independence of
G^{\prime}
and
(V,G)
, so for any measurable
\tilde{g}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\mathbb{E}\bigl(\tilde{g}(V+s_{2}G,G^{\prime})^{2}\bigr)<\infty
, we have
\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(V-g_{2}^{*}(V+s_{2}G)\bigr)\,\tilde{g}(V+s_{2}G,G^{\prime})\bigr\}=0
. We deduce from (
6.8
) that
\rho\mapsto\mmse(\rho)
is Lipschitz on
(\rho^{\prime},\infty)
for every
\rho^{\prime}>0
, and hence that it is continuous on
(0,\infty)
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
3.9
.

Given any sequence of functions
(g_{k})
for which the corresponding AMP iterations (
22
) satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem
3.1
or
3.5
, we prove (
40
) by induction on
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. For each such
k
, it follows from (
30
) and (
37
) that as
n\to\infty
, we have

\frac{\lvert\langle\hat{v}^{k},v\rangle_{n}\rvert}{\|\hat{v}^{k}\|_{n}\|v\|_{n}}\overset{c}{\to}\frac{\sqrt{\rho_{k+1}}}{\lambda}=\frac{\bigl|\mathbb{E}\bigl(Vg_{k}(\mu_{k}V+\sigma_{k}G_{k})\bigr)\bigr|}{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\bigl(g_{k}(\mu_{k}V+\sigma_{k}G_{k})^{2}\bigr)}}\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathbb{E}(V\,|\,\mu_{k}V+\sigma_{k}G_{k})^{2}\bigr)}=\sqrt{1-\mmse_{k}(\rho_{k})},

where we set
\rho_{0}\equiv\rho_{0}^{*}=(\mu_{0}/\sigma_{0})^{2}
and write
G_{0}
for the random variable
U
from
(M1)
when
k=0
. Now
\sqrt{1-\mmse_{0}(\rho_{0}^{*})}=\sqrt{\rho_{1}^{*}}/\lambda
by (
39
), so
\rho_{1}\leq\rho_{1}^{*}
and (
40
) holds when
k=0
. For a general
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\rho_{k}\leq\rho_{k}^{*}
by induction, so since
\rho\mapsto\mmse(\rho)\equiv\mmse_{k}(\rho)
is non-increasing by Corollary
6.19
, we deduce that
\sqrt{1-\mmse(\rho_{k})}\leq\sqrt{1-\mmse(\rho_{k}^{*})}=\sqrt{\rho_{k+1}^{*}}/\lambda
and hence that
\rho_{k+1}\leq\rho_{k+1}^{*}
. This completes the inductive step for (
40
).

As for (
41
), we can apply (
28
), the definition of
R_{\pi,\psi}(\rho)
, the fact that
\rho_{k}\leq\rho_{k}^{*}
and Lemma
3.7
(in that order) to conclude that
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(\hat{v}_{i}^{k},v_{i})\overset{c}{\to}\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(g_{k}(\mu_{k}V+\sigma_{k}G_{k}),V\bigr)\bigr\}\geq R_{\psi,\pi}(\rho_{k})\geq R_{\psi,\pi}(\rho_{k}^{*})
, as required.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
3.8
, each
g_{k}^{*}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz and satisfies
(M2)
, and by Corollary
6.19
,
\rho\mapsto\lambda^{2}\bigl(1-\mmse(\rho)\bigr)=:m_{\lambda}(\rho)
is non-decreasing on
[0,\infty)
and continuous on
(0,\infty)
.

(a) We will show that if either (i) or (ii) holds, then

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}\equiv\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}(\lambda):=\inf\{\rho>0:\rho=m_{\lambda}(\rho)\}>0,\qquad

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{*}\in[0,\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}],\qquad\rho_{1}^{*}>0,

(186)

\displaystyle m_{\lambda}(\rho)\geq\rho\;\text{for all }\rho\in[0,\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}],\qquad

\displaystyle\rho_{k+1}^{*}=m_{\lambda}(\rho_{k}^{*})\;\text{for all }k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Note that since
m_{\lambda}(\rho)\leq\lambda^{2}
for all
\rho\in[0,\infty)
, we always have
\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}(\lambda)\leq\lambda^{2}
.

(i)

Non-spectral initialisation
: In this case,
(M1)
holds with
\mu_{0}=0
,
\sigma_{0}=1
,
\rho_{0}^{*}=0
and
U=1
. Since
\mathbb{E}(V)\neq 0
and
\mathbb{E}(V^{2})=1
by
(M1)
,
\rho_{1}^{*}=\lambda^{2}\bigl(1-\mmse_{k}(0)\bigr)=\lambda^{2}\bigl(1-\Var(V)\bigr)=\lambda^{2}\,\mathbb{E}(V)^{2}=m_{\lambda}(0)>0
, and therefore
\rho_{k+1}^{*}=m_{\lambda}(\rho_{k}^{*})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. In addition,
m_{\lambda}(\rho)\geq m_{\lambda}(0)=\rho_{1}^{*}>\rho
for all
\rho\in[0,\rho_{1}^{*})
, so

\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}\geq\rho_{1}^{*}>0
and
m_{\lambda}(\rho)>\rho
for all
\rho\in[0,\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*})
.

(ii)

Spectral initialisation
: By Proposition
3.4
,
(M1)
holds with
U\equiv G_{0}\sim N(0,1)
, so
\rho_{k+1}^{*}=m_{\lambda}(\rho_{k}^{*})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Since
\mmse(\rho)
is the minimum value of
\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(V-g(\sqrt{\rho}V+G)\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}
as
g
ranges over all measurable functions,

m_{\lambda}(\rho)\geq\lambda^{2}\Bigl(1-\inf_{a,b\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(V-a(\sqrt{\rho}V+G)-b\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}\Bigr)=\lambda^{2}\,\biggl(1-\frac{1-\mathbb{E}(V)^{2}}{1+\rho\,\{1-\mathbb{E}(V)^{2}\}}\biggr)\geq\frac{\lambda^{2}\rho}{1+\rho}

for all
\rho\in[0,\infty)
, where the equality above can be verified by a routine calculation. Recalling that
\lambda>1
, we have
m_{\lambda}(\rho)\geq\lambda^{2}\rho/(1+\rho)>\rho
for all
\rho\in[0,\lambda^{2}-1)
, so
\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}\geq\lambda^{2}-1=\rho_{0}^{*}>0
and
m_{\lambda}(\rho)>\rho
for all
\rho\in(0,\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*})
.
Moreover,
\rho_{1}^{*}=m_{\lambda}(\rho_{0}^{*})\geq\rho_{0}^{*}>0
.

To complete the proof of (a), note that if
0\leq\rho_{k}^{*}\leq\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then by (
186
) and the fact that
\rho\mapsto m_{\lambda}(\rho)
is non-decreasing, we have
0\leq\rho_{k}^{*}\leq m_{\lambda}(\rho_{k}^{*})=\rho_{k+1}^{*}\leq m_{\lambda}(\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*})=\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}
. Since
\rho_{1}^{*}>0
, it follows by induction that
(\rho_{k}^{*})
is an increasing sequence that converges to some
\rho^{*}\in(0,\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}]
. By the continuity of
m_{\lambda}
on
(0,\infty)
, we conclude that
\rho^{*}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\rho_{k+1}^{*}=\lim_{k\to\infty}m_{\lambda}(\rho_{k}^{*})=m_{\lambda}(\rho^{*})
and hence that
\rho^{*}=\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}
.

(b) Since
g_{k,\psi}^{*}
is Lipschitz by assumption, this follows directly from Theorem
3.1
; see the proof of Corollary
3.2
.

(c) Since each
g_{k}^{*}
is Lipschitz and
\rho_{k}^{*}\nearrow\rho_{\mathrm{AMP}}^{*}
by (a), this is an immediate consequence of (
29
) and (
30
) from Corollary
3.2
.
∎

6.9
Proofs for Section
4

The proof of Lemma
4.1
makes use of the following multivariate version of Stein’s lemma.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be such that for
j=1,\dotsc,d
, the function
x_{j}\mapsto g(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{d})
is absolutely continuous for Lebesgue almost every
(x_{i}:i\neq j)\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, with weak derivative
D_{j}g\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}\bigl(\lvert D_{j}g(X)\rvert\bigr)<\infty
. Let
\nabla g(x):=(D_{1}(x),\dotsc,D_{d}(x))
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
X\sim N_{d}(0,\Sigma)
with
\Sigma
positive definite, then

\mathbb{E}\bigl(Xg(X)\bigr)=\Sigma\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(\nabla g(X)\bigr).

Proof.

The result for
\Sigma=I_{d}
is stated as
Tsybakov 2009
. For a general non-negative definite
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, let
\tilde{g}(z)=g(\Sigma^{1/2}z)
for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then
\nabla\tilde{g}(z)=\Sigma^{1/2}\,\nabla g(\Sigma^{1/2}z)
for all
z
(
Fourdrinier et al. 2018
, Theorem 2.1
;
Fan 2020
, Proposition E.5
), so by taking
Z\sim N_{d}(0,I_{d})
, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}\bigl(Xg(X)\bigr)=\Sigma^{1/2}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(Z\tilde{g}(Z)\bigr)=\Sigma^{1/2}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(\nabla\tilde{g}(Z)\bigr)=\Sigma\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(\nabla g(X)\bigr).

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

The first assertion follows from a general fact about Gaussian random vectors
(X_{1},X_{2})
, for which we write
\Sigma_{ij}:=\Cov(X_{i},X_{j})
for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
: if
\mathbb{E}(X_{1})=\mathbb{E}(X_{2})=0
and
\Sigma_{11}=\Var(X_{1})
is invertible, then
AX_{1}
and
X_{2}-AX_{1}
are uncorrelated and hence independent when
A:=\Sigma_{21}\Sigma_{11}^{-1}
. Thus,
(X_{1},X_{2})\overset{d}{=}(X_{1},AX_{1}+G)
, where
AX_{1}=\mathbb{E}(X_{2}\,|\,X_{1})
and
G\sim N(0,\Sigma_{22}-\Sigma_{21}\Sigma_{11}^{-1}\Sigma_{12})
is independent of
X_{1}
. For (
59
), we deduce from Lemma
6.20
that

\mathbb{E}\bigl(Z\tilde{g}_{k}(Z,Z_{k},v)\bigr)=\Sigma_{11}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(D_{1}\tilde{g}_{k}(Z,Z_{k},v)\bigr)+\Sigma_{12}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(D_{2}\tilde{g}_{k}(Z,Z_{k},v)\bigr)

for all
v\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\Sigma\equiv\Sigma_{k}
is as in (
58
). Thus, since
(Z,Z_{k})\overset{d}{=}(Z,\mu_{Z,k}Z+\sigma_{Z,k}\tilde{G}_{k})
is independent of
\bar{\varepsilon}
in (
57
), and
\mu_{Z,k}=\Sigma_{21}/\Sigma_{11}
by the first part of the lemma,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl(Zg_{k}(Z_{k},Y)\bigr)=\mathbb{E}\bigl(Z\tilde{g}_{k}(Z,Z_{k},\bar{\varepsilon})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{11}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(D_{1}\tilde{g}_{k}(Z,Z_{k},\bar{\varepsilon})\bigr)+\Sigma_{12}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(D_{2}\tilde{g}_{k}(Z,Z_{k},\bar{\varepsilon})\bigr)

(187)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}(\bar{\beta}^{2})}{\delta}\bigl\{\mu_{k+1}+\mu_{Z,k}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(g_{k}^{\prime}(Z_{k},Y)\bigr)\bigr\},

which yields the first equality. Next, by the tower property of expectation, the final expression in (
59
) can be written as

\mathbb{E}\biggl(\frac{\mathbb{E}(Z\,|\,Z_{k},Y)-\mathbb{E}(Z\,|\,Z_{k})}{\Var(Z\,|\,Z_{k})}\,g_{k}(Z_{k},Y)\biggr)=\mathbb{E}\biggl(\frac{Z-\mathbb{E}(Z\,|\,Z_{k})}{\Var(Z\,|\,Z_{k})}\,\tilde{g}_{k}(Z,Z_{k},\bar{\varepsilon})\biggr).

Since
Z
is conditionally Gaussian given
Z_{k}
and
\bar{\varepsilon}
is independent of
(Z,Z_{k})
, a further (conditional) application of Stein’s lemma yields

\mathbb{E}\biggl(\frac{Z-\mathbb{E}(Z\,|\,Z_{k})}{\Var(Z\,|\,Z_{k})}\,\tilde{g}_{k}(Z,Z_{k},\bar{\varepsilon})\biggm|Z_{k}\biggr)=\frac{\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(Z-\mathbb{E}(Z\,|\,Z_{k})\bigr)\,\tilde{g}_{k}(Z,Z_{k},\bar{\varepsilon})\!\bigm|\!Z_{k}\bigr\}}{\Var(Z\,|\,Z_{k})}=\mathbb{E}\bigl(D_{1}\tilde{g}_{k}(Z,Z_{k},\bar{\varepsilon})\!\bigm|\!Z_{k}\bigr),

so by taking expectations, we obtain the second identity for
\mu_{k+1}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.4
.

Consider the right hand side of (
82
) and write
\bar{J}(\tilde{\beta}):=\sum_{j=1}^{p}J(\tilde{\beta}_{j})
for
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Using the expression for the Lagrangian (
81
), and ignoring terms that do not depend on
\tilde{\beta}
, we obtain

\begin{split}\argmin_{\tilde{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\,\Bigl\{L(\tilde{\beta},\hat{\theta}^{k},\hat{s}^{k})-\frac{\bar{c}_{k}}{2}\|\tilde{\beta}-\hat{\beta}^{k}\|^{2}\Bigr\}&=\argmin_{\tilde{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\,\Bigl\{\bar{J}(\tilde{\beta})-\tilde{\beta}^{\top}X^{\top}\hat{s}^{k}-\frac{\bar{c}_{k}}{2}\|\tilde{\beta}-\hat{\beta}^{k}\|^{2}\Bigr\}\\
&=\argmin_{\tilde{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\,\biggl\{\bar{J}(\tilde{\beta})-\frac{\bar{c}_{k}}{2}\biggl\|\tilde{\beta}+\frac{X^{\top}\hat{s}^{k}-\bar{c}_{k}\hat{\beta}^{k}}{\bar{c}_{k}}\biggr\|^{2}\biggr\}\\
&=\argmin_{\tilde{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\,\biggl\{\bar{J}(\tilde{\beta})-\frac{\bar{c}_{k}}{2}\biggl\|\tilde{\beta}+\frac{\beta^{k+1}}{\bar{c}_{k}}\biggr\|^{2}\biggr\}=\hat{\beta}^{k+1},\end{split}

(188)

where the third and final equalities follow from the definitions of
\beta^{k+1}
and
\hat{\beta}^{k+1}=f_{k+1}(\beta^{k+1})
respectively in (
80
), with
f_{k+1}
as in (
75
). Similarly, in view of the definition of
\bar{g}_{k}
in (
74
), we can obtain (
83
) by completing the square. For (
84
), we can apply (
80
) to see that

\hat{s}^{k+1}=\frac{\hat{\theta}^{k+1}-\theta^{k+1}}{\bar{b}_{k+1}}=\frac{\hat{\theta}^{k+1}-(X\hat{\beta}^{k+1}-\bar{b}_{k+1}\hat{s}^{k})}{\bar{b}_{k+1}}=\hat{s}^{k}+\frac{(\hat{\theta}^{k+1}-X\hat{\beta}^{k+1})}{\bar{b}_{k+1}}.

(189)

For the final assertion of Proposition
4.4
, if
(\beta^{*},\theta^{*},\hat{\beta}^{*},\hat{\theta}^{*},\hat{s}^{*})
is a fixed point of the algorithm (
80
), then
\hat{\theta}^{*}=X\hat{\beta}^{*}
by (
84
), and (for example by considering subgradients) it follows from (
82
) and (
83
) respectively that

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}^{*}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\tilde{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\,L(\tilde{\beta},\hat{\theta}^{*},\hat{s}^{*})=\argmin_{\tilde{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\,\bigl\{\bar{J}(\tilde{\beta})-(X\tilde{\beta})^{\top}\hat{s}^{*}\bigr\},

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{*}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\tilde{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\,L(\hat{\beta}^{*},\tilde{\theta},\hat{s}^{*})=\argmin_{\tilde{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\,\bigl\{\bar{\ell}(\tilde{\theta},y)+\tilde{\theta}^{\top}\hat{s}^{*}\bigr\},

where
\bar{\ell}(\tilde{\theta},y):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\tilde{\theta}_{i},y_{i})
. Thus, for all
(\tilde{\beta},\tilde{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\tilde{\theta}=X\tilde{\beta}
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{J}(\tilde{\beta})+\bar{\ell}(\tilde{\theta},y)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\bar{J}(\tilde{\beta})-(X\tilde{\beta})^{\top}\hat{s}^{*}\bigr)+\bigl(\bar{\ell}(\tilde{\theta},y)+\tilde{\theta}^{\top}\hat{s}^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\bigl(\bar{J}(\hat{\beta}^{*})-(X\hat{\beta}^{*})^{\top}\hat{s}^{*}\bigr)+\bigl(\bar{\ell}(\hat{\theta}^{*},y)+(\hat{\theta}^{*})^{\top}\hat{s}^{*}\bigr)=\bar{J}(\hat{\beta}^{*})+\bar{\ell}(\hat{\theta}^{*},y),

so
(\hat{\beta}^{*},\hat{\theta}^{*})
is a solution to the optimisation problem (
73
), as required.
∎

7
Supplementary mathematical background

7.1
Basic properties of complete convergence

Proof of Proposition
1.2
.

For (a), suppose that
\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(\|X_{n}\|_{E}>\varepsilon)<\infty
for all
\varepsilon>0
. Then for any sequence
(Y_{n})
of
E
-valued random elements with
Y_{n}\overset{d}{=}X_{n}
for all
n
, the first Borel–Cantelli lemma implies that
\mathbb{P}(\|Y_{n}\|_{E}>\varepsilon\text{ infinitely often})=0
for all
\varepsilon>0

and hence that
Y_{n}\to 0
almost surely. This shows that
X_{n}\overset{c}{\to}0
. Conversely, suppose that
\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(\|X_{n}\|_{E}>\varepsilon)=\infty
for some
\varepsilon>0
. Then for a sequence
(Y_{n})
of independent
E
-valued random elements with
Y_{n}\overset{d}{=}X_{n}
for all
n
, the second Borel–Cantelli lemma implies that
\mathbb{P}(\|Y_{n}\|_{E}>\varepsilon\text{ infinitely often})=1
and hence that
Y_{n}\nrightarrow 0
almost surely. Thus,
X_{n}\not\overset{c}{\to}0
.

The argument for (b) is similar. If
\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(\|X_{n}\|_{E}>C)<\infty
for all
C>0
, then
X_{n}=O_{c}(1)
by the first Borel–Cantelli lemma. Conversely, suppose that
\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(\|X_{n}\|_{E}>C)=\infty
for all
C>0
. Then for a sequence
(Y_{n})
of independent
E
-valued random elements with
Y_{n}\overset{d}{=}X_{n}
for all
n
, the second Borel–Cantelli lemma implies that
\mathbb{P}(\|Y_{n}\|_{E}>C\text{ infinitely often})=1
for all
C>0

and hence that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|Y_{n}\|_{E}=\infty
almost surely. Thus,
(X_{n})
is not
O_{c}(1)
.
∎

Remark 7.1
.

For a random sequence
(X_{n})
taking values in a Euclidean space
(E,\|\cdot\|_{E})
, it can be seen from Definition
1.1
and Proposition
1.2
that complete convergence (to a degenerate limit) is a property of the marginal distributions of the random elements
X_{1},X_{2},\dotsc
and not of their joint dependence structure (i.e. the specific coupling between them), so
X_{1},X_{2},\dotsc
need not be defined on the same probability space. Thus, just as for weak convergence or convergence in probability to a degenerate limit (but not almost sure convergence), there is a meaningful notion of complete convergence
for sequences
(\mu_{n})
of Borel probability measures on
E
: defining
\bar{B}(x,\varepsilon):=\{x^{\prime}\in E:\|x^{\prime}-x\|_{E}\leq\varepsilon\}
for
\varepsilon>0
, we write
\mu_{n}\overset{c}{\to}\delta_{x}
if
\sum_{n}\mu_{n}\bigl(\bar{B}(x,\varepsilon)^{c}\bigr)<\infty
for all
\varepsilon>0
.

Example 1
.

Let
(X_{n})
be any sequence of random variables for which there exist
c_{1},c_{2},\beta>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(\lvert X_{n}\rvert>t)\leq c_{1}\exp(-c_{2}t^{\beta})
for all
t>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(a_{n})
be a deterministic sequence of real numbers. If
a_{n}=o(1)
, then clearly
a_{n}X_{n}=o_{p}(1)
, and if
a_{n}=O(1)
, then
a_{n}X_{n}=O_{p}(1)
. Moreover:

(a)

If
\lvert a_{n}\rvert^{\beta}\log n\to 0
, then for every
t>0
, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
c_{2}(t/\lvert a_{n}\rvert)^{\beta}\geq 2\log n
for all
n>N
, so
\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(\lvert a_{n}X_{n}\rvert>t)\leq N+\sum_{n>N}c_{1}e^{-2\log n}<\infty
. Thus,
a_{n}X_{n}=o_{c}(1)
by Proposition
1.2
(a).

(b)

If
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,\lvert a_{n}\rvert^{\beta}\log n<\infty
, then there exists
t>0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
c_{2}(t/\lvert a_{n}\rvert)^{\beta}\geq 2\log n
for all
n>N
, so
\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(\lvert a_{n}X_{n}\rvert>t)<\infty
as in (i). Thus,
a_{n}X_{n}=O_{c}(1)
by Proposition
1.2
(b).

Suppose in addition that there exist
c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(\lvert X_{n}\rvert>t)\geq c_{1}^{\prime}\exp(-c_{2}^{\prime}t^{\beta^{\prime}})
for all
t>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(c)

If
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\,\lvert a_{n}\rvert^{\beta^{\prime}}\log n>0
, then there exist
t>0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
c_{2}^{\prime}(t/\lvert a_{n}\rvert)^{\beta^{\prime}}\leq\log n
for all
n>N
, so
\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(\lvert a_{n}X_{n}\rvert>t)\geq\sum_{n\geq N}c_{1}e^{-\log n}=\infty
. Thus,
(a_{n}X_{n})
is not
o_{c}(1)
in view of Proposition
1.2
(a).

(d)

If
\lvert a_{n}\rvert^{\beta^{\prime}}\log n\to\infty
, then for every
t>0
, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
c_{2}^{\prime}(t/\lvert a_{n}\rvert)^{\beta^{\prime}}\leq\log n
for all
n>N
, so
\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(\lvert a_{n}X_{n}\rvert>t)=\infty
as in (iii). Thus,
(a_{n}X_{n})
is not
O_{c}(1)
in view of Proposition
1.2
(b).

For instance, suppose that
X_{n}=X\sim N(0,1)
for all
n
. Then
X_{n}\to X
almost surely and
X_{n}=O_{p}(1)
but
(X_{n})
is not
O_{c}(1)
, and
X_{n}/\log^{1/2}n\to 0
almost surely but
(X_{n}/\log^{1/2}n)
is not
o_{c}(1)
.

Using Proposition
1.2
, it is straightforward to verify that the continuous mapping theorem and Slutsky’s lemma remain valid when stated in terms of complete convergence.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
(X_{n}),(Y_{n})
be sequences of random elements taking values in Euclidean spaces
E,E^{\prime}
respectively such that
X_{n}\overset{c}{\to}x
and
Y_{n}\overset{c}{\to}y
for some deterministic limits
x\in E
and
y\in E^{\prime}
. Then
(X_{n},Y_{n})\overset{c}{\to}(x,y)
in
E\times E^{\prime}

and
g(X_{n})\overset{c}{\to}g(x)
in
E^{\prime}
for any function
g\colon E\to E^{\prime}
that is continuous at
x
. If in addition
x
lies in some open set
U\subseteq E
, then
\mathbbm{1}_{\{X_{n}\notin U\}}\overset{c}{\to}0
.

Consequently,
X_{n}+Y_{n}\overset{c}{\to}x+y
when
E=E^{\prime}
. Moreover,
X_{n}Y_{n}\overset{c}{\to}xy
when
E=\mathbb{R}
and
E^{\prime}
is any Euclidean space (in the case of scalar multiplication), or when
E=\mathbb{R}^{k\times\ell}
and
E^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
for some
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
(in the case of matrix multiplication). If in addition
k=\ell
and
x^{-1}\in E
is well-defined, then
\mathbbm{1}_{\{X_{n}\text{ is not invertible}\}}\overset{c}{\to}0
and
X_{n}^{+}Y_{n}\overset{c}{\to}x^{-1}y
.

Proof.

For the first part of the lemma, we apply Proposition
1.2
(a). Since
\|(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\|_{E\times E^{\prime}}^{2}=\|\tilde{x}\|_{E}^{2}+\|\tilde{y}\|_{E^{\prime}}^{2}
for any
(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\in E\times E^{\prime}
, we have

\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|(X_{n},Y_{n})-(x,y)\|_{E\times E^{\prime}}>\varepsilon\bigr)\leq\sum_{n}\,\bigl\{\mathbb{P}(\|X_{n}-x\|_{E}>\varepsilon/\sqrt{2})+\mathbb{P}(\|Y_{n}-y\|_{E^{\prime}}>\varepsilon/\sqrt{2})\bigr\}<\infty

for all
\varepsilon>0
, so
(X_{n},Y_{n})\overset{c}{\to}(x,y)
by Proposition
1.2
(a). If
g\colon E\to E^{\prime}
is continuous at
x\in E
, then for each
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
\|g(\tilde{x})-g(x)\|_{E^{\prime}}<\varepsilon
whenever
\|\tilde{x}-x\|_{E}<\delta
, so

\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(\|g(X_{n})-g(x)\|_{E^{\prime}}>\varepsilon)\leq\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(\|X_{n}-x\|_{E}>\delta)<\infty.

This holds for all
\varepsilon>0
, so
g(X_{n})\overset{c}{\to}g(x)
by Proposition
1.2
(a). When
x
lies in some open set
U\subseteq E
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(X_{n}\notin U)\leq\mathbb{P}(\|X_{n}-X\|_{E}>\varepsilon)
, so
\mathbbm{1}_{\{X_{n}\notin U\}}\overset{c}{\to}0
, again by Proposition
1.2
(a).

Having established the first part of the lemma, we can now apply the facts above to deduce the remaining assertions. Indeed, when
E=E^{\prime}
, the function
g\colon(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\mapsto\tilde{x}+\tilde{y}
is continuous on
E\times E^{\prime}
and we know that
(X_{n},Y_{n})\overset{c}{\to}(x,y)
, so it follows that
X_{n}+Y_{n}\overset{c}{\to}x+y
. When
E=\mathbb{R}
or when
E=\mathbb{R}^{k\times\ell}
and
E^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
for some
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, the scalar and matrix multiplication maps (respectively) are continuous on
E\times E^{\prime}
. Therefore, it follows similarly that
X_{n}Y_{n}\overset{c}{\to}xy
.

If in addition
k=\ell
, then
\tilde{x}^{+}=\tilde{x}^{-1}
for all invertible
\tilde{x}\in E=\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
, so the map
\tilde{x}\mapsto\tilde{x}^{+}
is continuous on the set
U
of all invertible
\tilde{x}\in E
, which is open. A further application of the continuous mapping result above shows that if
x
is invertible, then
\mathbbm{1}_{\{X_{n}\text{ is not invertible}\}}\overset{c}{\to}0
and
X_{n}^{+}Y_{n}\overset{c}{\to}x^{-1}y
, as required.
∎

Remark 7.3
.

By a similar application of Proposition
1.2
, it can be shown that the stochastic
o_{c}
and
O_{c}
symbols obey the ‘arithmetic rules’ of standard
O
notation. Written in compact form, some examples of these are as follows (for sequences defined on spaces with compatible dimensions):

\displaystyle O_{c}(1)+O_{c}(1)

\displaystyle=O_{c}(1),\qquad\quad

\displaystyle o_{c}(1)+o_{c}(1)

\displaystyle=o_{c}(1),\qquad\quad

\displaystyle O_{c}(1)+o_{c}(1)

\displaystyle=O_{c}(1),

(190)

\displaystyle O_{c}(1)\,O_{c}(1)

\displaystyle=O_{c}(1),\qquad\quad

\displaystyle o_{c}(1)\,o_{c}(1)

\displaystyle=o_{c}(1),\qquad\quad

\displaystyle O_{c}(1)\,o_{c}(1)

\displaystyle=O_{c}(1),

where the assertions in the second line apply to scalar multiplication or matrix multiplication as appropriate. (The proofs are straightforward and are therefore omitted.) To give another example of a basic fact that follows directly from Definition
1.1
or Proposition
1.2
, let
E,E^{\prime}
be Euclidean spaces and suppose that
g\colon E\to E^{\prime}
is bounded on every bounded subset of
E
. Then for any sequence
(X_{n})
of
E
-valued random elements such that
X_{n}=O_{c}(1)
, we also have
g(X_{n})=O_{c}(1)
.

7.2
Regular conditional distributions and conditional independence

First, we recall the notion of conditional expectation: if
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is a probability space and
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
is a sub-
\sigma
-algebra, we write
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
for the restricted probability measure on
(\Omega,\mathcal{G})
given by
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}(B):=\mathbb{P}(B)
for
B\in\mathcal{G}
. If
Y\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a random variable with
\mathbb{E}(|Y|)<\infty
, then there exists a
\mathcal{G}
-measurable random variable
Z=\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,\mathcal{G})
with the property that
\mathbb{E}(Z\mathbbm{1}_{E})=\mathbb{E}(Y\mathbbm{1}_{E})
for all
E\in\mathcal{G}

(
Dudley 2002
, Chapter 10.1)
. We call
Z
the
conditional expectation of
Y
given
\mathcal{G}
, noting that it is unique up to
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost sure equivalence. For
F\in\mathcal{F}
, we also write
\mathbb{P}(F\,|\,\mathcal{G}):=\mathbb{E}(\mathbbm{1}_{F}\,|\,\mathcal{G})
.

If
X
is a measurable function from
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
to a measurable space
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
, we say that
P_{X|\mathcal{G}}\colon\Omega\times\mathcal{A}\to[0,1]
is a
(regular) conditional distribution for
X
given
\mathcal{G}
if

(i)

for every

\omega\in\Omega
, the set function
P_{X|\mathcal{G}}(\omega,\cdot)
is a probability measure on
\mathcal{A}
;

(ii)

for each
A\in\mathcal{A}
, the map
P_{X|\mathcal{G}}(\cdot,A)
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable, and
P_{X|\mathcal{G}}(\omega,A)=\mathbb{P}(X^{-1}(A)\,|\,\mathcal{G})(\omega)
for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, so that
\mathbb{P}(X^{-1}(A)\cap E)=\int_{E}P_{X|\mathcal{G}}(\omega,A)\,d\mathbb{P}(\omega)
for all
E\in\mathcal{G}
.

We say that
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
is a
Borel space
if there exist a Borel subset
S\subseteq[0,1]
(equipped with the restriction
\mathcal{B}_{S}
of the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
[0,1]
to
S
) and a bijection
f\colon(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})\to(S,\mathcal{B}_{S})
such that both
f
and
f^{-1}
are measurable. Examples of Borel spaces
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
include
Polish spaces
(i.e. separable, completely metrisable topological spaces)

\mathscr{X}
equipped with their Borel
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{A}

(
Kallenberg 1997
, Theorem A1.6)
.

Whenever
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
is a Borel space, there exists a conditional distribution
P_{X|\mathcal{G}}
, and moreover, if
P^{\prime}
is another such conditional distribution, then
P^{\prime}(\omega,\cdot)=P_{X|\mathcal{G}}(\omega,\cdot)
for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
; see
Kallenberg 1997
and
Dudley 2002
. For brevity, we will write ‘
X
has conditional distribution
P\equiv P_{\omega}
given
\mathcal{G}
on an event
\Omega_{0}\in\mathcal{G}
’ to mean that there exists a conditional distribution
P_{X|\mathcal{G}}
, and we can take
P_{X|\mathcal{G}}(\omega,\cdot)=P_{\omega}(\cdot)
for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
. When we omit the phrase ‘on an event
\Omega_{0}\in\mathcal{G}
’, we mean that the statement holds for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
.

For measurable
X,X^{\prime}\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
, we say that
X,X^{\prime}
are
identically distributed given
\mathcal{G}
, and write
X\mathrel{\overset{d}{=}\!|_{\mathcal{G}}}X^{\prime}
, if there exist conditional distributions
P_{X|\mathcal{G}}
and
P_{X^{\prime}|\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
for
X,X^{\prime}
respectively, and
P_{\omega}(\cdot)\equiv P_{X|\mathcal{G}}(\omega,\cdot)=P_{X^{\prime}|\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}(\omega,\cdot)\equiv P_{\omega}^{\prime}(\cdot)
for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
.

Remark 7.4
.

For example,
X
has distribution
Q
on
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
and is independent of
\mathcal{G}
if and only if
X
has conditional distribution
P_{\omega}=Q
for all
\omega\in\Omega
.

Remark 7.5
.

Let
X\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
be as above and consider the important special case where
\mathcal{G}=\sigma(Y)
for some measurable map
Y
from
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
to a measurable space
(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
. Denote by
P
the joint distribution of
(X,Y)\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{X}\times\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})
and by
P^{Y}
the (marginal) distribution of
Y
on
(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
. We note here that a random variable
Z\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to\mathbb{R}
is
\sigma(Y)
-measurable if and only if
Z=g\circ Y
for some measurable function
g\colon(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})\to\mathbb{R}
(i.e. ‘
Z(\omega)
depends on
\omega
only through
Y(\omega)
’); see for example
Dudley 2002
.
Using this fact and the defining property (ii) above, it can be verified
(as in
Dudley 2002
, Theorem 10.2.1)
that there exists a regular conditional distribution
P_{X|\sigma(Y)}\colon\Omega\times\mathcal{A}\to[0,1]
if and only if there is a family of probability distributions
(Q_{y})_{y\in\mathscr{Y}}
on
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
such that the following hold for every
A\in\mathcal{A}
:

(I)

y\mapsto Q_{y}(A)
is a measurable function from
(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
to
\mathbb{R}
;

(II)

P(A\times B)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(X^{-1}(A)\cap Y^{-1}(B)\bigr)=\int_{B}Q_{y}(A)\,dP^{Y}(y)
for all
B\in\mathcal{B}
.

In this case, for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\sigma(Y)}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, we have
P_{X|\sigma(Y)}(\omega,A)=Q_{Y(\omega)}(A)
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
. Note that
(Q_{y})_{y\in\mathscr{Y}}
is only unique up to
P^{Y}
-almost sure equivalence, in the sense that if
(Q_{y}^{\prime})_{y\in\mathscr{Y}}
satisfies (I) and
Q_{y}=Q_{y}^{\prime}
for
P^{Y}
-almost every
y\in\mathscr{Y}
, then
(Q_{y}^{\prime})_{y\in\mathscr{Y}}
also satisfies (II). In view of (I), the map
(y,A)\mapsto Q_{y}(A)
is said to be a
probability kernel
. An interpretation of (II) is that it makes precise the notion of
disintegrating
the joint distribution
P
of
(X,Y)
into the marginal distribution
P^{Y}
of
Y
and the distributions
(Q_{y})_{y\in\mathscr{Y}}
, where (for
P^{Y}
-almost every
y\in\mathscr{Y}
) we can view
Q_{y}
as the “conditional distribution of
X
given
Y=y
”. Indeed, by analogy with the construction of the usual product measure and Fubini’s theorem
(e.g.
Dudley 2002
, Chapter 4.4)
, it can be shown that if
\phi\colon(\mathscr{X}\times\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\to\mathbb{R}
is
P
-integrable (i.e.
\phi
is measurable and
\mathbb{E}\bigl(\lvert\phi(X,Y)\rvert\bigr)<\infty
), then

(III)

x\mapsto\phi(x,y)
is
\mathcal{A}
-measurable for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and
Q_{y}
-integrable for
P^{Y}
-almost every
y\in\mathcal{Y}
;

(IV)

y\mapsto\int_{\mathscr{X}}\phi(x,y)\,dQ_{y}(x)
is
\mathcal{B}
-measurable and
P^{Y}
-integrable;

(V)

\int_{\mathscr{X}\times\mathscr{Y}}\,\phi(x,y)\,dP(x,y)=\int_{\mathscr{Y}}\,\bigl(\int_{\mathscr{X}}\phi(x,y)\,dQ_{y}(x)\bigr)\,dP^{Y}(y)
.

This generalisation of Fubini’s theorem is sometimes known as the
disintegration theorem
, and is derived from (II) using a monotone class argument; see
Dudley 2002
and
Kallenberg 1997
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A}),(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
be measurable spaces and let
(\mathscr{Z},\mathcal{C})
be a Borel space.
Let
\phi\colon(\mathscr{X}\times\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\to(\mathscr{Z},\mathcal{C})
be a measurable function and let
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
be a
\sigma
-algebra.

(a)

If
E\in\mathcal{G}
and
X_{1},X_{2}\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
are measurable functions with conditional distributions
P\equiv P_{\omega}
and
Q\equiv Q_{\omega}
respectively given
\mathcal{G}
, then the measurable function
X\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
satisfying
X=X_{1}
on
E
and
X=X_{2}
on
E^{c}
has conditional distribution
R(\cdot)\equiv R_{\omega}(\cdot):=P_{\omega}(\cdot)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\omega\in E\}}+Q_{\omega}(\cdot)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\omega\in E^{c}\}}
given
\mathcal{G}
.

(b)

For
D\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
and
y\in\mathscr{Y}
, let
D^{y}:=\{x\in\mathscr{X}:(x,y)\in D\}=\iota_{y}^{-1}(D)
, where
\iota_{y}\colon\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{X}\times\mathscr{Y}
denotes the map
x\mapsto(x,y)
. Fix
\Omega_{0}\in\mathcal{G}
. Suppose that
X\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
has conditional distribution
P\equiv P_{\omega}
given
\mathcal{G}
on
\Omega_{0}
, and that
Y\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable. If
Z\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\rightarrow(\mathscr{Z},\mathcal{C})
is a measurable map that agrees with
\phi(X,Y)
on
\Omega_{0}
, then
Z
has conditional distribution
\tilde{P}\equiv\tilde{P}_{\omega}=P_{\omega}\circ(\phi\circ\iota_{Y(\omega)})^{-1}
given
\mathcal{G}
on
\Omega_{0}
, so that
\tilde{P}_{\omega}(C)=P_{\omega}\bigl(\phi^{-1}(C)^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)
for all
C\in\mathcal{C}
and
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
.

(c)

Suppose that
X,X^{\prime}\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
are measurable functions satisfying
X\mathrel{\overset{d}{=}\!|_{\mathcal{G}}}X^{\prime}
, and that
Y\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable. Then
\phi(X,Y)\mathrel{\overset{d}{=}\!|_{\mathcal{G}}}\phi(X^{\prime},Y)
.

The result in (b) has an intuitive interpretation. Suppose for simplicity that
\Omega_{0}=\Omega
, and fix
\omega\in\Omega
. Let
\mu:=P_{\omega}
be taken from the conditional distribution of
X
given
\mathcal{G}
, and assume that
Y
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable. To obtain the corresponding
\tilde{P}_{\omega}
from the conditional distribution of
\phi(X,Y)
given
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
7.6
(b) tells us that we can take
\tilde{P}_{\omega}
to be the distribution of
\phi(U,y)
, where
U\sim\mu
and
y:=Y(\omega)
. In essence, the reason for this is that since
Y
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable, we can think of
Y
as being ‘fixed’ once we have conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

(a) The fact that
R_{\omega}(\cdot)
is a probability measure on
\mathcal{A}
for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
follows immediately from the corresponding facts for
P_{\omega}(\cdot)
and
Q_{\omega}(\cdot)
. For each
A\in\mathcal{A}
, the map
\omega\mapsto R_{\omega}(A)
is a composition of
\mathcal{G}
-measurable functions (since
E\in\mathcal{G}
by assumption), so is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable.

For
A\in\mathcal{A}
, let
\chi_{A}\colon\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
denote the indicator function of
A
. Then

\chi_{A}\circ X=(\chi_{A}\circ X_{1})\mathbbm{1}_{E}+(\chi_{A}\circ X_{2})\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}.

Since
\mathbbm{1}_{E}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}
are
\mathcal{G}
-measurable, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(X^{-1}(A)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl((\chi_{A}\circ X_{1})\mathbbm{1}_{E}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)+\mathbb{E}\bigl((\chi_{A}\circ X_{2})\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\chi_{A}\circ X_{1}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{E}(\omega)+\mathbb{E}\bigl((\chi_{A}\circ X_{2})\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=P_{\omega}(A)\mathbbm{1}_{E}(\omega)+Q_{\omega}(A)\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}(\omega)=R_{\omega}(A)

for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, as required.

(b) This can be deduced from
Kallenberg 1997
and part (a) above, but we give a direct proof here for completeness. Note that for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
, the set function
\tilde{P}_{\omega}
is the push-forward (image measure) of
P_{\omega}
induced by the measurable map
x\mapsto\phi\circ\iota_{Y(\omega)}(x)
from
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
to
(\mathscr{Z},\mathcal{C})
; thus,
\tilde{P}_{\omega}
is indeed a probability measure for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
.

Now let
\mathcal{D}
denote the collection of all
D\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
for which
\omega\mapsto P_{\omega}\bigl(D^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable and
P_{\omega}\bigl(D^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(D)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)
for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
. If
D=A\times B
for some
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
, then
D^{Y(\omega)}=A
if
Y(\omega)\in B
, and
D^{Y(\omega)}=\emptyset
if
Y(\omega)\notin B
. Thus,

\displaystyle P_{\omega}\bigl(D^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{\omega}(A)\mathbbm{1}_{\{Y(\omega)\in B\}}=\mathbb{P}\bigl(X^{-1}(A)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)\mathbbm{1}_{\{Y(\omega)\in B\}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\chi_{A}\circ X\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)\cdot(\chi_{B}\circ Y)(\omega)=\mathbb{E}\bigl((\chi_{A}\circ X)\cdot(\chi_{B}\circ Y)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(D)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)

for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
, where we have used the fact that
\chi_{B}\circ Y
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable in the penultimate equality. Thus
\mathcal{D}\supseteq\{A\times B:A\in\mathcal{A},\,B\in\mathcal{B}\}
, which is a
\pi
-system that generates
\mathcal{D}
. Now suppose that
D_{1},D_{2}\in\mathcal{D}
with
D_{1}\subseteq D_{2}
. Then

\displaystyle P_{\omega}\bigl((D_{2}\setminus D_{1})^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{\omega}\bigl(D_{2}^{Y(\omega)}\setminus D_{1}^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)=P_{\omega}\bigl(D_{2}^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)-P_{\omega}\bigl(D_{1}^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(D_{2})\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)-\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(D_{1})\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(D_{2}\setminus D_{1})\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)

for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
, so
D_{2}\setminus D_{1}\in\mathcal{D}
. Finally, let
(D_{n})
be an increasing sequence of sets in
\mathcal{D}
, and let
D:=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}D_{n}
. Then
D^{Y(\omega)}=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}D_{n}^{Y(\omega)}
and
(X,Y)^{-1}(D)=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}(X,Y)^{-1}(D_{n})
, so that

\displaystyle P_{\omega}\bigl(D^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}P_{\omega}\bigl(D_{n}^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(D_{n})\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)=\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(D)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)

for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
