\omega\in\Omega_{0}
, where we have used the conditional monotone convergence theorem in the final equality
(
Dudley 2002
, Theorem 10.1.7)
. Thus,
D\in\mathcal{D}
, and it follows from Dynkin’s lemma that
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
.

Finally, if
C\in\mathcal{C}
, then
D:=\phi^{-1}(C)\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
, so that for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
,

\displaystyle P_{\omega}\bigl(\phi^{-1}(C)^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{\omega}\bigl(D^{Y(\omega)}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(D)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(D)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{0}}(\omega)=\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(D)\cap\Omega_{0}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl(Z^{-1}(C)\cap\Omega_{0}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(Z^{-1}(C)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega),

as required, since
\Omega_{0}\in\mathcal{G}
.

(c) This follows directly from (b) on setting
\Omega_{0}=\Omega
.
∎

The following useful result is a special case of
Kallenberg 1997
and can be derived
using the definition of conditional expectation
(
Dudley 2002
, Problem 10.1.9)
, or alternatively using regular conditional distributions and standard measure-theoretic devices (similarly to the proofs of Lemma
7.6
(b) above and
Dudley 2002
).

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
X,Y
be measurable functions from
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
to measurable spaces
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A}),(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
respectively, and let
\phi\colon(\mathscr{X}\times\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function satisfying
\mathbb{E}\bigl(\lvert\phi(X,Y)\rvert\bigr)<\infty
. Let
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
be a
\sigma
-algebra, and suppose that
Y
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable. If
X
has distribution
Q
on
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
and is independent of
\mathcal{G}
, then
\mathbb{E}\bigl(\phi(X,Y)\!\bigm|\!\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)=\int_{\mathscr{X}}\phi\bigl(x,Y(\omega)\bigr)\,dQ(x)
for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
.

Next, for
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{2},\mathcal{G}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
, we say that
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
are
conditionally independent given
\mathcal{G}_{3}
, and write
\mathcal{G}_{1}\mathrel{\perp\!\!\!\perp}\mathcal{G}_{2}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{3}
, if
\mathbb{P}(A_{1}\cap A_{2}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{3})=\mathbb{P}(A_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{3})\,\mathbb{P}(A_{2}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{3})
almost surely for all
A_{1}\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
A_{2}\in\mathcal{G}_{2}
, or equivalently if
\mathbb{P}\bigl(A_{1}\,|\,\sigma(\mathcal{G}_{2},\mathcal{G}_{3})\bigr)=\mathbb{P}(A_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{3})
almost surely for all
A_{1}\in\mathcal{G}_{1}

(
Kallenberg 1997
, Proposition 5.6)
. If this holds with
\mathcal{G}_{1}=\sigma(X)
for some random variable
X
, we also say that
X
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{G}_{3}
, and write
X\mathrel{\perp\!\!\!\perp}\mathcal{G}_{2}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{3}
(and similarly for
\mathcal{G}_{2}
and
\mathcal{G}_{3}
). The following basic facts follow straightforwardly from the definition of conditional independence.

Lemma 7.8

(
Kallenberg 1997
, Corollary 5.7(i)
)
.

We have
\mathcal{G}_{1}\mathrel{\perp\!\!\!\perp}\mathcal{G}_{2}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{3}
if and only if
\sigma(\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{3})\mathrel{\perp\!\!\!\perp}\mathcal{G}_{2}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{3}
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
and
(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
be measurable spaces and let
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
be a
\sigma
-algebra.

(a)

For
i=1,2
, let
X_{i}\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
and
Y_{i}\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
be measurable functions such that
X_{i}\mathrel{\perp\!\!\!\perp}Y_{i}\,|\,\mathcal{G}
. For
E\in\mathcal{G}
, let
X\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
be the measurable function satisfying
X=X_{1}
on
E
and
X=X_{2}
on
E^{c}
, and define
Y
similarly. Then
X\mathrel{\perp\!\!\!\perp}Y\,|\,\mathcal{G}
.

(b)

Suppose that the measurable maps
X\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
and
Y\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
have conditional distributions
P\equiv P_{\omega}
and
Q\equiv Q_{\omega}
respectively given
\mathcal{G}
. Then
X\mathrel{\perp\!\!\!\perp}Y\,|\,\mathcal{G}
if and only if
(X,Y)\colon(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\to(\mathscr{X}\times\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})
has conditional distribution
R\equiv R_{\omega}:=P_{\omega}\otimes Q_{\omega}
given
\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

(a) For fixed
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
, write
\chi_{A}\colon(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})\to\{0,1\}
and
\chi_{B}\colon(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})\to\{0,1\}
for the respective indicator functions, and for
i=1,2
, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl((\chi_{A}\circ X_{i})\cdot(\chi_{B}\circ Y_{i})\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(X_{i}^{-1}(A)\cap Y_{i}^{-1}(B)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl(X_{i}^{-1}(A)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)\mathbb{P}\bigl(Y_{i}^{-1}(B)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\chi_{A}\circ X_{i}\,|\,\mathcal{G})\,\mathbb{E}(\chi_{B}\circ Y_{i}\,|\,\mathcal{G})

almost surely, since
X_{i}\mathrel{\perp\!\!\!\perp}Y_{i}\,|\,\mathcal{G}
. As in the proof of Lemma
7.6
(a), we have
\chi_{A}\circ X=(\chi_{A}\circ X_{1})\mathbbm{1}_{E}+(\chi_{A^{c}}\circ X_{2})\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}
and
\chi_{B}\circ Y=(\chi_{B}\circ Y_{1})\mathbbm{1}_{E}+(\chi_{B^{c}}\circ Y_{2})\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}
, so it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(X^{-1}(A)\cap Y^{-1}(B)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl((\chi_{A}\circ X)\cdot(\chi_{B}\circ Y)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl((\chi_{A}\circ X_{1})\cdot(\chi_{B}\circ Y_{1})\mathbbm{1}_{E}+(\chi_{A}\circ X_{2})\cdot(\chi_{B}\circ Y_{2})\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl((\chi_{A}\circ X_{1})\cdot(\chi_{B}\circ Y_{1})\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{E}+\mathbb{E}\bigl((\chi_{A}\circ X_{2})\cdot(\chi_{B}\circ Y_{2})\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\chi_{A}\circ X_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{G})\,\mathbb{E}(\chi_{B}\circ Y_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{G})\mathbbm{1}_{E}+\mathbb{E}(\chi_{A}\circ X_{2}\,|\,\mathcal{G})\,\mathbb{E}(\chi_{B}\circ Y_{2}\,|\,\mathcal{G})\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl((\chi_{A}\circ X_{1})\mathbbm{1}_{E}+(\chi_{A}\circ X_{2})\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)\,\mathbb{E}\bigl((\chi_{B}\circ Y_{1})\mathbbm{1}_{E}+(\chi_{B}\circ Y_{2})\mathbbm{1}_{E^{c}}\,|\,\mathcal{G}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\chi_{A}\circ X\,|\,\mathcal{G})\,\mathbb{E}(\chi_{B}\circ Y\,|\,\mathcal{G})=\mathbb{P}\bigl(X^{-1}(A)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)\,\mathbb{P}\bigl(Y^{-1}(B)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr),

where we have used the fact that
E\in\mathcal{G}
to obtain the third-last equality. Since this holds for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
, the result follows.

(b) For
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(X^{-1}(A)\cap Y^{-1}(B)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)=\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(A\times B)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)

(191)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(X^{-1}(A)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)\cdot\mathbb{P}\bigl(Y^{-1}(B)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)=P_{\omega}(A)\,Q_{\omega}(B)=R_{\omega}(A\times B)

(192)

for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
. Thus, if
(X,Y)
has conditional distribution
R\equiv R_{\omega}=P_{\omega}\otimes Q_{\omega}
given
\mathcal{G}
, then for any
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
, the right hand sides of (
191
) and (
192
) agree for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, so the same is true of the left hand sides. This shows that
X\mathrel{\perp\!\!\!\perp}Y\,|\,\mathcal{G}
.

Conversely, suppose that
X\mathrel{\perp\!\!\!\perp}Y\,|\,\mathcal{G}
and let
\mathcal{D}
be the collection of all
D\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
such that
R_{\omega}(D)=(P_{\omega}\otimes Q_{\omega})(D)=\mathbb{P}\bigl((X,Y)^{-1}(D)\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)(\omega)
for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
. Then for any
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
, the left hand sides of (
191
) and (
192
) agree for
\left.\mathbb{P}\right|_{\mathcal{G}}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, so
\mathcal{D}
contains a
\pi
-system
\{A\times B:A\in\mathcal{A},\,B\in\mathcal{B}\}
that generates
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
. Similarly to the proof of Lemma
7.6
(b), it can be verified that
\mathcal{D}
is a
d
-system,
so it follows from Dynkin’s lemma that
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
, and hence that
(X,Y)
has conditional distribution
R\equiv R_{\omega}=P_{\omega}\otimes Q_{\omega}
, as required.
∎

7.3
Auxiliary probabilistic results

The following general result is used in the proofs of some important complete convergence statements in Sections
6.4
and
7.4
, specifically Proposition
6.16
(g) and Corollary
7.21
(b).

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
(X_{n}),(Y_{n})
be sequences of random elements defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
such that
X_{n},Y_{n}
take values in Polish spaces
E_{n},E_{n}^{\prime}
respectively for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and suppose that there exist Borel measurable functions
g_{n}\colon E_{n}\to E_{n}^{\prime}
such that
Y_{n}\overset{d}{=}g_{n}(X_{n})
for each
n
. Then there exists a sequence of random elements
\tilde{X}_{n}\colon\Omega\to E_{n}
such that
\tilde{X}_{n}\overset{d}{=}X_{n}
for all
n
and
\bigl(g_{1}(\tilde{X}_{1}),g_{2}(\tilde{X}_{2}),\dotsc\bigr)=(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dotsc)
almost surely (viewed as random sequences taking values in
\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}E_{n}^{\prime}
, equipped with its cylindrical (i.e. Borel)
\sigma
-algebra).

This is an extension to random sequences of the following result for pairs of random elements: given random elements
X_{1},X_{2}
taking values in
E_{1},E_{2}
respectively, let
(Y_{1},Y_{2})\sim\pi
be any coupling of
g_{1}(X_{1}),g_{2}(X_{2})
. Then there exists a coupling
(X_{1}^{\prime},X_{2}^{\prime})\sim\pi^{\prime}
of
X_{1},X_{2}
such that
\bigl(g_{1}(X_{1}^{\prime}),g_{2}(X_{2}^{\prime})\bigr)\overset{d}{=}(Y_{1},Y_{2})
, i.e.
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\circ(g_{1},g_{2})^{-1}
. This can be proved by applying the gluing lemma from optimal transport
(
Villani 2003
, Lemma 7.6)
or a simpler version of the general argument below.

Given an arbitrary coupling
(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dotsc)
of the random elements
g_{1}(X_{1}),g_{2}(X_{2}),\dotsc
, the first (and most important) step in the proof below is to ‘lift’ this to produce a suitable coupling
(X_{1}^{\prime},X_{2}^{\prime},\dotsc)
of the random elements
X_{1},X_{2},\dotsc
, in such a way that
\bigl(g_{1}(X_{1}^{\prime}),g_{2}(X_{2}^{\prime}),\dotsc\bigr)\overset{d}{=}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dotsc)
as random sequences. Intuitively, the key construction can be interpreted as the output of the following two-stage procedure:

(A)

Denoting by
\pi
the (given) distribution of
(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dotsc)
on
\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}E_{n}^{\prime}
, we first draw
(Y_{1}^{\prime},Y_{2}^{\prime},\dotsc)\sim\pi
;

(B)

Having obtained
(Y_{1}^{\prime},Y_{2}^{\prime},\dotsc)=(y_{1},y_{2},\dotsc)
from Step A, we then generate
X_{1}^{\prime},X_{2}^{\prime},\dotsc
by sampling independently from
Q_{y_{1}}^{1},Q_{y_{2}}^{2},\dotsc
, where
Q_{y_{n}}^{n}
denotes the “conditional distribution of
X_{n}
given
g_{n}(X_{n})=y_{n}
”.

Step B ensures that
X_{1}^{\prime},X_{2}^{\prime},\dotsc
are conditionally independent given
(Y_{1}^{\prime},Y_{2}^{\prime},\dotsc)
. To make rigorous sense of this informal description and to validate the construction, we use the language of
disintegration of measures
, as outlined in Remark
7.5
. There are similarities here with the proof of the gluing lemma
(
Villani 2003
, Lemma 7.6)
.
To verify that the random sequences
\bigl(g_{n}(X_{n}^{\prime})\bigr)
and
(Y_{n})
have the same distribution on
\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}E_{n}^{\prime}
, it suffices to show that they have the same finite-dimensional distributions, i.e. that
\bigl(g_{1}(X_{1}^{\prime}),\dotsc,g_{n}(X_{n}^{\prime})\bigr)\overset{d}{=}(Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{n})
for all
n
. Finally, to upgrade all the distributional equalities above to almost-sure equalities, we appeal to a general result from abstract probability theory
(
Kallenberg 1997
, Corollary 5.11)
,
which is also proved using disintegration techniques.

Remark 7.11
.

To guarantee the existence of a random sequence
(\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{X}_{2},\dotsc)
with a given distribution on
\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}E_{n}^{\prime}
, we require the underlying probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
to be rich enough to support a sequence of independent
U[0,1]
random variables. This can be assumed without loss of generality, since otherwise we can work with the
product space
(\Omega\times[0,1],\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]},\mathbb{P}\otimes\mu_{[0,1]})
, where
\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
and
\mu_{[0,1]}
denote the Borel
\sigma
-algebra and Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
respectively.

Proof of Lemma
7.10
.

For each
n
, denote by
\mathcal{B}_{n},\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\prime}
the Borel
\sigma
-algebras of
E_{n},E_{n}^{\prime}
respectively. It follows from
Dudley 2002
and
Kallenberg 1997
that
\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}^{\prime}
and
\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}
are Polish spaces with Borel
\sigma
-algebras
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\prime}
and
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{j}
respectively. Denote by
\mu_{n},\pi_{n}
the distributions of
Y_{n}
and
(Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{n})
on
(E_{n}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\prime})
and
\bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}^{\prime},\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\prime}\bigr)
respectively. Since
E_{n}
is a Polish space, we know from Section
7.2
that there exists a regular conditional distribution for
X_{n}
given
\sigma\bigl(g_{n}(X_{n})\bigr)
. Equivalently, there is a family of probability distributions
(Q_{y}^{n})_{y\in E_{n}^{\prime}}
on
E_{n}
satisfying conditions (I) and (II) in Remark
7.5
, where we take
X:=X_{n}
,
Y:=g_{n}(X_{n})\overset{d}{=}Y_{n}
and
P^{Y}:=\mu_{n}
. It follows from Remark
7.5
(II) that
\mathbb{P}(X_{n}\in A)=\int_{E_{n}^{\prime}}Q_{y}^{n}(A)\,d\mu_{n}(y)
for all
A\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, and moreover that

\int_{B^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)\,d\mu_{n}(y)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(g_{n}(X_{n})\in B\cap B^{\prime}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(X_{n}\in g_{n}^{-1}(B),\,g_{n}(X_{n})\in B^{\prime}\bigr)=\int_{B^{\prime}}Q_{y}^{n}\bigl(g_{n}^{-1}(B)\bigr)\,d\mu_{n}(y)

(193)

for
B,B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\prime}
. Thus, for all
B\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\prime}
, we have
Q_{y}^{n}\bigl(g_{n}^{-1}(B)\bigr)=\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)
for
\mu_{n}
-almost every
y\in E_{n}^{\prime}
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we now define a new measure
\pi_{n}^{\prime}
on
\bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_{j},\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{j}\bigr)
by

\pi_{n}^{\prime}(A):=\int_{\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}^{\prime}}\biggl(\,\int_{E_{1}}\dotsi\int_{E_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\,dQ_{y_{n}}^{n}(x_{n})\;\dotsi\,dQ_{y_{1}}^{1}(x_{1})\biggr)\,d\pi_{n}(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{n})

(194)

for
A\in\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{j}
. That this a well-defined probability measure follows from Remark
7.5
(III, IV)
and the monotone convergence theorem. For each
n
, we claim that

(i)

\pi_{n+1}^{\prime}(A\times E_{n+1})=\pi_{n}^{\prime}(A)
for every
A\in\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{j}
;

(ii)

\mathbb{P}(X_{n}\in A_{n})=\pi_{n}^{\prime}\bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}E_{j}\times A_{n}\bigr)
for every
A_{n}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
;

(iii)

\pi_{n}=\pi_{n}^{\prime}\circ(g_{1},\dotsc,g_{n})^{-1}
, where
(g_{1},\dotsc,g_{n})\colon\bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_{j},\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{j}\bigr)\to\bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}^{\prime},\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\prime}\bigr)
denotes the measurable map
(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\mapsto\bigl(g_{1}(x_{1}),\dotsc,g_{n}(x_{n})\bigr)
.

Property (i) is immediate from (
194
) and the fact that
\pi_{n+1}(B\times E_{n}^{\prime})=\pi_{n}(B)
for all
B\in\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\prime}
. To verify (ii), observe that

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{\prime}\bigl(\textstyle\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}E_{j}\times A_{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}^{\prime}}\,\int_{E_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}(x_{n})\,dQ_{y_{n}}^{n}(x_{n})\,d\pi_{n}(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{n})

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{n}^{\prime}}Q_{y_{n}}^{n}(A_{n})\,d\mu_{n}(y_{n})=\mathbb{P}(X_{n}\in A_{n}),

where the final equality is obtained from Remark
7.5
(II) as above. As for (iii), fix
B_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\prime}
for
1\leq j\leq n
and note that by (
193
) and (
194
), we have

\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{\prime}\bigl(\textstyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}g_{j}^{-1}(B_{j})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}^{\prime}}Q_{y_{1}}^{1}\bigl(g_{1}^{-1}(B_{1})\bigr)\dotsm\,Q_{y_{n}}^{n}\bigl(g_{n}^{-1}(B_{n})\bigr)\,d\pi_{n}(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{n})

\displaystyle=\int_{\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{1}}(y_{1})\dotsm\mathbbm{1}_{B_{n}}(y_{n})\,d\pi_{n}(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{n})=\pi_{n}\bigl(\textstyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}B_{j}\bigr).

This means that
\pi_{n}
and
\pi_{n}^{\prime}\circ(g_{1},\dotsc,g_{n})^{-1}
agree on
\bigl\{\prod_{j=1}^{n}B_{j}:B_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\prime}\text{ for all }1\leq j\leq n\bigr\}
, a
\pi
-system that generates
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{j}
, so (iii) holds.

Since the distributions
\pi_{1}^{\prime},\pi_{2}^{\prime},\dotsc
on the Polish spaces
E_{1},E_{1}\times E_{2},\dotsc
satisfy the consistency condition (i), we deduce from the Daniell–Kolmogorov extension theorem
(
Kallenberg 1997
, Theorem 5.14)
and Remark
7.11
that exists a sequence
(X_{n}^{\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of random elements
X_{n}^{\prime}\colon\Omega\to E_{n}
such that
(X_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,X_{n}^{\prime})\sim\pi_{n}^{\prime}
on
\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_{j}
for each
n
. Then by (ii) and (iii) above, we have
X_{n}^{\prime}\overset{d}{=}X_{n}
and
\bigl(g_{1}(X_{1}^{\prime}),\dotsc,g_{n}(X_{n}^{\prime})\bigr)\sim\pi_{n}^{\prime}\circ(g_{1},\dotsc,g_{n})^{-1}=\pi_{n}
for each
n
, where
\pi_{n}
was defined to be the distribution of
(Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{n})
. Thus, the sequences
\bigl(g_{n}(X_{n}^{\prime})\bigr)
and
(Y_{n})
have the same finite-dimensional distributions; in other words, their distributions agree on
\bigl\{\prod_{j=1}^{N}B_{j}\times\prod_{j=N+1}^{\infty}E_{j}^{\prime}:N\in\mathbb{N},\,B_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\prime}\text{ for all }1\leq j\leq N\bigr\}
, a collection of cylindrical sets that generate the cylindrical
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
of
\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}E_{n}^{\prime}
. (By
Kallenberg 1997
,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}E_{n}^{\prime}
.)
We conclude that
\bigl(g_{1}(X_{1}^{\prime}),g_{2}(X_{2}^{\prime}),\dotsc\bigr)\overset{d}{=}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dotsc)
as random sequences taking values in
\bigl(\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}E_{n}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\bigr)
.

Finally, we apply
Kallenberg 1997
with
T=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}E_{n}
,
S=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}E_{n}^{\prime}
,
\eta=(X_{n}^{\prime})
,
\xi=(Y_{n})

and
f\colon T\to S
given by
f(x_{1},x_{2},\dotsc)=\bigl(g_{1}(x_{1}),g_{2}(x_{2}),\dotsc\bigr)
; note that
T,S
are Polish spaces
(e.g.
Dudley 2002
, Theorem 2.5.7)

and that
f
is Borel measurable. Having already shown that
f(\eta)\overset{d}{=}\xi
, we deduce from
Kallenberg 1997
that there exists
(\tilde{X}_{n})\equiv\tilde{\eta}\overset{d}{=}\eta=(X_{n}^{\prime})
satisfying
\bigl(g_{n}(\tilde{X}_{n})\bigr)=f(\tilde{\eta})=\xi=(Y_{n})
almost surely, as required.
∎

In the proofs of Proposition
6.16
(a, c), we apply the concentration inequality below for sums of pseudo-Lipschitz functions of independent Gaussian random variables.

Lemma 7.12
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that the following holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
r\geq 2
and
t\geq 0
: if
Z_{1},\dotsc,Z_{n}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(0,1)
,
L\equiv(L_{1},\dotsc,L_{n})\in(0,\infty)^{n}
and
f_{i}\in\mathrm{PL}_{1}(r,L_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq n
, then

\mathbb{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\bigl\{f_{i}(Z_{i})-\mathbb{E}\bigl(f_{i}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigr\}\biggr|\geq t\biggr)\leq\exp\biggl(1-\min\,\biggl\{\biggl(\frac{nt}{(Cr)^{r}\|L\|_{2}}\biggr)^{2},\biggl(\frac{nt}{(Cr)^{r}\|L\|_{\infty}}\biggr)^{2/r}\biggr\}\biggr).

(195)

Proof.

We first consider the case
n=1
. For arbitrary
r\geq 2
and
L>0
, we may assume without loss of generality that
f\equiv f_{1}\in\mathrm{PL}(r,L)
satisfies
f(0)=0
, so that
\lvert f(x)\rvert=\lvert f(x)-f(0)\rvert\leq L(\lvert x\rvert+\lvert x\rvert^{r})\leq 2L(\lvert x\rvert\vee\lvert x\rvert^{r})
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus, if
Z\sim N(0,1)
, then

\mathbb{P}(\lvert f(Z)\rvert\geq s)\leq\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert Z\rvert\vee\lvert Z\rvert^{r}\geq s/(2L)\bigr)\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\left(\frac{s}{2L}\right)^{2},\,\left(\frac{s}{2L}\right)^{2/r}\right\}}

(196)

for all
s\geq 0
, and

\frac{\mathbb{E}\bigl(\lvert f(Z)\rvert\bigl)}{L}\leq\mathbb{E}(\lvert Z\rvert+\lvert Z\rvert^{r})=\left(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}+\frac{2^{r/2}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\,\Gamma\left(\frac{r+1}{2}\right)\right)=:\upsilon_{r}

by direct computation. Now
\Gamma(x)<e^{1/(12x)}(x/e)^{x}\sqrt{2\pi/x}
for all
x>0
by a non-asymptotic version of Stirling’s formula; see for example
Gordon 1994
and
Dümbgen et al. 2021
. Since
r\geq 2
, we have
(r+1)/e<r
and
(\sqrt{2}-1)r^{r/2}\geq 2(\sqrt{2}-1)>1/\sqrt{\pi}
. Therefore,

\frac{\upsilon_{r}}{2}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}+\left(\frac{r+1}{e}\right)^{r/2}e^{\frac{1}{6(r+1)}-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}+r^{r/2}\right)<r^{r/2}.

(197)

Thus, for
t\geq L\upsilon_{r}
, we deduce from (
196
) and (
197
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl\{\bigl|f(Z)-\mathbb{E}\bigl(f(Z)\bigr)\bigr|\geq t\bigr\}\leq\mathbb{P}\bigl\{\lvert f(Z)\rvert\geq t-\mathbb{E}\bigl(\lvert f(Z)\rvert\bigl)\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\left(\frac{t}{2L}-\frac{\upsilon_{r}}{2}\right)^{2},\,\left(\frac{t}{2L}-\frac{\upsilon_{r}}{2}\right)^{2/r}\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{t}{2L}-\frac{\upsilon_{r}}{2}\right)^{2/r}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{1+r}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{t}{2L}\right)^{2/r}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{1-\left(\frac{t}{2L}\right)^{2/r}\frac{1}{r+1}},

(198)

where the third inequality follows from the fact that
a^{2/r}\leq\lvert a-b\rvert^{2/r}+b^{2/r}
for
r\geq 2
as above and any
a,b\geq 0
. Now (
198
) holds trivially for all
t\in[0,L\upsilon_{r})
since
1-(r+1)^{-1}\{t/(2L)\}^{2/r}>1-(r+1)^{-1}(\upsilon_{r}/2)^{2/r}>0
by (
197
), so (
195
) holds with
C=3
when
n=1
.

We now derive (
195
) for general
n\geq 2
with the aid of Theorem 3.1 and Proposition A.3 in
Kuchibhotla and Chakrabortty 2018
; see also Theorem 1 and Corollary 2 in
Bakhshizadeh et al. 2020
. As in Sections 2 and 3 of
Kuchibhotla and Chakrabortty 2018
, we begin by defining
\vartheta_{\beta}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
for each
\beta>0
by
\vartheta_{\beta}(x):=\exp(x^{\beta})-1
.
Moreover, for
\beta,\lambda>0
, let
\vartheta_{\beta,\lambda}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be the continuous, strictly increasing function with inverse given by
\vartheta_{\beta,\lambda}^{-1}(t):=\log^{1/2}(1+t)+\lambda\log^{1/\beta}(1+t)
for
t\geq 0
. For a random variable
X
and a strictly increasing function
g\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
satisfying
g(0)=0
, we write
\Xi_{g}(X):=\inf\bigl\{\theta>0:\mathbb{E}\bigl(g(\lvert X\rvert/\theta)\bigr)\leq 1\bigr\}\in[0,\infty]
, setting
\inf\emptyset=\infty
by convention. Note that
\Xi_{g}(X)
is precisely the
g
-Orlicz norm of
X
when
g
is convex, but that
\Xi_{g}
does not in general define a norm when
g
is not convex (for example when
g=\vartheta_{\beta}
for
\beta\in(0,1)
, as in the proof below).

For arbitrary
n\geq 2
,
r\geq 2
and
L\equiv(L_{1},\dotsc,L_{n})\in(0,\infty)^{n}
, let
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{n}\in\mathrm{PL}(r,L_{i})
and
Z_{1},\dotsc,Z_{n}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(0,1)
, and assume without loss of generality that
X_{i}:=f_{i}(Z_{i})
satisfies
\mathbb{E}(X_{i})=0
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Setting
\beta:=2/r\in[0,1]
and
\theta_{i}:=2\{4(r+1)\}^{r/2}\,L_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
, we now integrate up the bound (
198
) to see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl(\vartheta_{\beta}(\lvert X_{i}\rvert/\theta_{i})\bigr)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\vartheta_{\beta}(\lvert X_{i}\rvert/\theta_{i})\geq t\bigr)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geq\theta_{i}\vartheta_{\beta}^{-1}(t)\bigr)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geq 2(r+1)^{r/2}L_{i}\,\{4\log(1+t)\}^{1/\beta}\bigr)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}e(1+t)^{-4}\,dt=e/3<1,

(199)

whence
\Xi_{\vartheta_{\beta}}(X_{i})\leq\theta_{i}=2\{4(r+1)\}^{r/2}\,L_{i}<\infty
. This shows that
X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}
are independent, centred
sub-Weibull
random variables of order
\beta=2/r
, in the sense of Definition 2.2 in
Kuchibhotla and Chakrabortty 2018
. Then applying
Kuchibhotla and Chakrabortty 2018
with
a=(1/n,\dotsc,1/n)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
b:=\bigl(\Xi_{\vartheta_{\beta}}(X_{1})/n,\dotsc,\Xi_{\vartheta_{\beta}}(X_{n})/n\bigr)
in their notation, we deduce from (
199
) that

\Xi_{\vartheta_{\beta,\lambda_{\beta}}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\biggr)\leq 2eC_{\beta}\|b\|_{2},\text{ where }\begin{cases}C_{\beta}:=(2e^{2/e}/\beta)^{1/\beta}(128\pi)^{1/4}\,e^{3+\frac{1}{24}}\\
\lambda_{\beta}:=(4^{1/\beta}/\sqrt{2})\,\|b\|_{\infty}/\|b\|_{2}.\end{cases}

(200)

It then follows from Proposition A.3 in
Kuchibhotla and Chakrabortty 2018
that

\mathbb{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\biggr|\geq 4eC_{\beta}\|b\|_{2}\max\bigl(s^{1/2},\lambda_{\beta}s^{1/\beta}\bigr)\biggr)\leq e^{1-s}

for all
s\geq 0
, and hence that

\mathbb{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\biggr|\geq t\biggr)\leq\exp\biggl(1-\min\biggl\{\biggl(\frac{t}{4eC_{\beta}\|b\|_{2}}\biggr)^{2},\biggl(\frac{t}{4eC_{\beta}^{\prime}\|b\|_{\infty}}\biggr)^{\beta}\biggr\}\biggr)

for all
t\geq 0
, where
C_{\beta}^{\prime}:=(4^{1/\beta}/\sqrt{2})\,C_{\beta}
. Since
\beta=2/r
and
\Xi_{\vartheta_{\beta}}(X_{i})\leq 2\{4(r+1)\}^{r/2}\,L_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
, we have

n\|b\|_{p}=\bigl\|\bigl(\Xi_{\vartheta_{\beta}}(X_{1}),\dotsc,\Xi_{\vartheta_{\beta}}(X_{n})\bigr)\bigr\|_{p}\leq 2\{4(r+1)\}^{r/2}\,\|L\|_{p}

for
p\in\{2,\infty\}
. Moreover,
2\{4(r+1)\}^{r/2}\,C_{\beta}\leq 2\{4(r+1)\}^{r/2}\,C_{\beta}^{\prime}\lesssim\{4e^{1/e}(r+1)\}^{r}
, so we can indeed find a suitable universal constant
C>0
in (
195
) such that the desired conclusion holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
r\geq 2
,
L\equiv(L_{1},\dotsc,L_{n})\in(0,\infty)^{n}
and
t\geq 0
, as required.
∎

Remark 7.13
.

When
r>2
,
f\in\mathrm{PL}_{1}(r)
and
Z\sim N(0,1)
, the moment generating function of
f(Z)
may not be finite anywhere except at 0 if
f(Z)
has heavier tails than an exponential random variable (for example when
f(z)=\sgn(z)\lvert z\rvert^{r}
for
z\in\mathbb{R}
). In these situations, the standard Chernoff method fails, which is why we apply different techniques that can handle general sub-Weibull random variables.

While we are primarily concerned with the case
r\geq 2
in the proof of Proposition
6.16
, there is an analogue of (
195
) when
r\in[1,2)
, namely

\mathbb{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\bigl\{f_{i}(Z_{i})-\mathbb{E}\bigl(f_{i}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigr\}\biggr|\geq t\biggr)\leq\exp\biggl(1-\min\,\biggl\{\biggl(\frac{nt}{C\|L\|_{2}}\biggr)^{2},\biggl(\frac{nt}{C\|L\|_{\tilde{r}}}\biggr)^{2/r}\biggr\}\biggr),

(201)

where
C>0
is a suitable universal constant and
\tilde{r}:=2/(2-r)\in[2,\infty)
is the Hölder conjugate of
2/r
. This can be proved using a Chernoff bound
(e.g.
Boucheron et al. 2013
, Exercise 2.27)
, or alternatively using
Kuchibhotla and Chakrabortty 2018
once again, where we instead take
\beta:=2/r
,
C_{\beta}:=4e+2(2\log 2)^{r/2}
and
\lambda_{\beta}:=(4^{1+1/\beta}C_{\beta}^{-1}e/\sqrt{2})\,\|b\|_{\tilde{r}}/\|b\|_{2}
in (
200
).

The proof of Proposition
6.16
(g) makes use of the following straightforward consequence of the definition of weak convergence.

Lemma 7.14
.

On a Euclidean space
E
, if
(\mu_{n})
is a sequence of Borel probability measures that converges weakly to a Borel probability measure
\mu
, then
\int_{E}g\,d\mu_{n}\to\int_{E}g\,d\mu
for any bounded, Borel measurable
g\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
that is continuous
\mu
-almost everywhere (in the sense that the set of discontinuities of
g
has
\mu
-measure 0).

Proof.

Writing
A\subseteq E
for the set of discontinuities of
g
, we have
\mu(A)=0
by assumption. By Skorokhod’s representation theorem
(e.g.
Kallenberg 1997
, Theorem 3.30)
, there exist random variables
X,X_{1},X_{2},\dotsc
defined on a common probability space such that
X\sim\mu
,
X_{n}\sim\mu_{n}
for all
n
and
X_{n}\to X
almost surely. Then
g(X_{n})\to g(X)
almost surely on the event
\{X\in A^{c}\}
, which has probability
\mu(A^{c})=1
, so an application of the dominated (or bounded) convergence theorem shows that
\int_{E}g\,d\mu_{n}=\mathbb{E}\bigl(g(X_{n})\bigr)\to\mathbb{E}\bigl(g(X)\bigr)=\int_{E}g\,d\mu
, as required.
∎

Remark 7.15
.

For each Lipschitz function
f_{k}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
in the AMP recursion (
5
), we assume in
(A5)
that there exists some
f_{k}^{\prime}
that
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
7.14
above with
\mu=\lambda\otimes\pi
; recall that
\lambda
denotes Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
and the probability distribution
\pi
is as in
(A1)
. To see why
(A5)
is a non-vacuous (albeit very mild) condition, consider Borel probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
of the form
\mu=\lambda\otimes\nu
, where
D\geq 2

and
\nu
is some probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{D-1}
. We will now give an example of a Lipschitz function
G\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
whose partial derivative
\frac{\partial G}{\partial x_{1}}
cannot be extended beyond its domain of definition to a function
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
that is continuous
\mu
-almost everywhere, for any
\mu
of the above form.

Denote by
C\subseteq[0,1]
the
fat Cantor set

(e.g.
Aliprantis and Burkinshaw 1998
, pp. 140–141)
, which has the property that for all
x\in C
and
\varepsilon>0
, both
(x-\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)\cap C
and
(x-\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)\cap C^{c}
have positive Lebesgue measure.
Then for any
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with
f=\mathbbm{1}_{C}
Lebesgue almost everywhere, we have
\{f(u):u\in(x-\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)\}=\{0,1\}
for all
x\in C
and
\varepsilon>0
, so
f
is discontinuous on
C
, which has Lebesgue measure
1/2>0
.
Note that
F\colon x\mapsto\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathbbm{1}_{C}(t)\,dt
is a Lipschitz function on
\mathbb{R}
with
F^{\prime}(x)=\mathbbm{1}_{C}(x)
for Lebesgue almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus, for general
D\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
G\colon(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{D})\mapsto F(x_{1})
is Lipschitz on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, and if
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
agrees with
\frac{\partial G}{\partial x_{1}}
everywhere where the latter is defined, then
g
is discontinuous on
C\times\mathbb{R}^{D-1}
,
which has strictly positive
\mu
-measure when
\mu=\lambda\otimes\nu
as above.

7.4
Wasserstein convergence and pseudo-Lipschitz functions

Throughout this subsection, we fix
D\in\mathbb{N}
and
r\in[1,\infty)
, and write
\mathcal{P}(r)\equiv\mathcal{P}_{D}(r)
for the set of probability measures
P
on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\|x\|^{r}\,dP(x)<\infty
(i.e. a finite
r^{th}
moment). For
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(r)
, recall from Section
1.1
the definitions of
\widetilde{d}_{r}(P,Q)
and the
r
-Wasserstein distance
d_{r}(P,Q)
.

The primary purpose of this subsection is to establish Theorem
7.17
and its probabilistic Corollary
7.21
, which can be viewed as extensions of
Villani 2003
. These show in particular that
\widetilde{d}_{r}
and
d_{r}
are metrics on
\mathcal{P}(r)
that induce the same topology (Remark
7.18
), and also formalise the link between functions in
\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
and convergence in
d_{r}
(or equivalently
\widetilde{d}_{r}
).

As a first step towards the proof of Theorem
7.17
, it is helpful to establish the following.

Proposition 7.16
.

There exists a countable set
T_{r}^{\prime}
of bounded Lipschitz functions on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
with the property that
\widetilde{d}_{r}(P,Q)=\sup_{\psi\in T_{r}^{\prime}}\,\bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\psi\,dP-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\psi\,dQ\bigr|\in[0,\infty)
for all
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(r)
.

A key property of the set
T_{r}^{\prime}
we construct is that for any
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
, there exists a sequence
(\psi_{\ell})
in
T_{r}^{\prime}
that converges uniformly to
\psi
on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{D}
. In subsequent proofs, we will write
Q(f)
as shorthand for
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}f\,dQ
when
Q
is a signed Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
and
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
Q
-integrable function.

Proof.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
B_{N}\equiv\bar{B}_{D}(0,N):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}:\|x\|\leq N\}
and define
f_{N}(x):=(N-\|x\|)\vee 0\wedge 1
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, so that
f_{N}
is 1-Lipschitz on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
,
f_{N}=1
on
B_{N-1}
and
f_{N}=0
on
B_{N}^{c}
. In the argument below (and in the proof of Theorem
7.17
), we will use
f_{N}
as a substitute for the (discontinuous) indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N}}
in several places. Note in particular that if
\tilde{g}\colon B_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz on
B_{N}
, then the function
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
g(x):=\tilde{g}(x)f_{N}(x)
is Lipschitz and supported on the compact set
B_{N}
.

Recalling the definitions of
\widetilde{d}_{r},\mathcal{P}(r)
from (
4
) and writing
\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)
for the set of all
\phi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r,1)
satisfying
\phi(0)=0
, we see from (
3
) that

\widetilde{d}_{r}(P,Q)=\sup_{\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)}\lvert(P-Q)(\phi)\rvert\leq\sup_{\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)}(P+Q)(\lvert\phi\rvert)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}(\|x\|+\|x\|^{r})\,d(P+Q)(x)<\infty

(202)

for all
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(r)
. If
\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)
, then

\bigl|\phi(x)-\phi(y)\bigr|\leq\|x-y\|\bigl(1+\|x\|^{r-1}+\|y\|^{r-1}\bigr)\leq(1+2N^{r-1})\|x-y\|

for all
x,y\in B_{N}
, so
\left.\phi\right|_{B_{N}}
belongs to the set of
(1+2N^{r-1})
-Lipschitz functions
g\colon B_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
g(0)=0
, which we denote by
\mathcal{G}_{N}
. Since
B_{N}
is compact and
\mathcal{G}_{N}
is uniformly bounded and equicontinuous,
\mathcal{G}_{N}
is therefore compact for the supremum norm on
B_{N}
by the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem
(e.g.
Dudley 2002
, Theorem 2.4.7)
. It is therefore totally bounded, so for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we can find a finite subset
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N,m}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{N}
such that for any
g\in\mathcal{G}_{N}
, there exists
\tilde{h}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N,m}
with
\sup_{x\in B_{N}}|g(x)-\tilde{h}(x)|<1/m
. Each
\tilde{h}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N,m}
can be associated with a function
h\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
h(x):=\tilde{h}(x)f_{N}(x)
. By the reasoning in the previous paragraph, the collection
\mathcal{H}_{N,m}
of all such
h
is a finite set of bounded Lipschitz functions supported on
B_{N}
.

Consequently,
T_{r}^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{N,m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{H}_{N,m}
is a countable set of bounded Lipschitz functions on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, and we claim that this has the desired property that
\widetilde{d}_{r}(P,Q)=\sup_{\psi\in T_{r}^{\prime}}\,\lvert(P-Q)(\psi)\rvert
for any two probability measures
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(r)
. Indeed, for fixed
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(r)
, the function
\psi_{r}\colon x\mapsto\|x\|+\|x\|^{r}
is integrable with respect to both
P
and
Q
on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, so by the dominated convergence theorem, we have
P\bigl(\psi_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N-1}^{c}}\bigr)\to 0
and
Q\bigl(\psi_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N-1}^{c}}\bigr)\to 0
as
N\to\infty
. Thus, for an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a sufficiently large
N\equiv N_{\varepsilon,r}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
P\bigl(\psi_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N-1}^{c}}\bigr)<\varepsilon/4
and
Q\bigl(\psi_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N-1}^{c}}\bigr)<\varepsilon/4
. Choosing
m\equiv m_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
1/m<\varepsilon/4
, we deduce from the previous paragraph that for any
\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)
, there exists
\tilde{h}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N,m}
such that
\sup_{x\in B_{N}}|\phi(x)-\tilde{h}(x)|<1/m<\varepsilon/4
. Letting
h
be the corresponding function in
\mathcal{H}_{N,m}\subseteq T_{r}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert(P-Q)(\phi)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert(P-Q)\bigl(\phi(1-f_{N})\bigr)\rvert+\lvert(P-Q)(\phi f_{N}-h)\rvert+\lvert(P-Q)(h)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq(P+Q)\bigl(\lvert\phi\rvert(1-f_{N})\bigr)+(P+Q)(\lvert\phi f_{N}-h\rvert)+\textstyle\sup_{\psi\in T_{r}^{\prime}}\lvert(P-Q)(\psi)\rvert

(203)

by the triangle inequality. Since
\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)
, we have
\lvert\phi(x)\rvert=\lvert\phi(x)-\phi(0)\rvert\leq\|x\|+\|x\|^{r}=\psi_{r}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, whence

\displaystyle(P+Q)\bigl(\lvert\phi\rvert(1-f_{N})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq(P+Q)\bigl(\lvert\phi\rvert\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N-1}^{c}}\bigr)\leq(P+Q)\bigl(\psi_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N-1}^{c}}\bigr)<\varepsilon/2

by our choice of
N
and the fact that
0\leq 1-f_{N}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N-1}^{c}}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle(P+Q)(\lvert\phi f_{N}-h)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2\textstyle\sup_{x\in B_{N}}|\phi(x)f_{N}(x)-h(x)|\leq 2\textstyle\sup_{x\in B_{N}}|\phi(x)-\tilde{h}(x)|<2/m<\varepsilon/2

by our choice of
h
, so it follows from (
203
) that
\lvert(P-Q)(\phi)\rvert<\varepsilon+\sup_{\psi\in T_{r}^{\prime}}\,\lvert(P-Q)(\psi)\rvert
. Since this holds for every
\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)
and all
\varepsilon>0
, the result follows.
∎

Theorem 7.17
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{D}(r)
and let
(P_{n})
be a sequence of probability measures in
\mathcal{P}_{D}(r)
. Then there exists a countable set
T_{r}\subseteq\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
such that the following are equivalent:

(i)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\psi\,dP_{n}\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\psi\,dP
for all
\psi\in T_{r}
;

(ii)

\widetilde{d}_{r}(P_{n},P)\to 0
;

(iii)

d_{r}(P_{n},P)\to 0
.

A suitable set
T_{r}\subseteq\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
can be constructed by enlarging the set
T_{r}^{\prime}
of bounded Lipschitz functions defined in (the proof of) Proposition
7.16
.

Remark 7.18
.

Using Theorem
7.17
, we can verify that
\widetilde{d}_{r}
is a metric on
\mathcal{P}(r)\equiv\mathcal{P}_{D}(r)
that generates the same topology as
d_{r}
. Indeed, it is clear from (
4
) and (
202
) that
\widetilde{d}_{r}
takes values in
[0,\infty)
and satisfies the triangle inequality on
\mathcal{P}(r)
. In addition, if
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(r)
are such that
\widetilde{d}_{r}(P,Q)=0
, then by taking
P_{n}=Q
for all
n
in (ii) above, we deduce that
d_{r}(P,Q)=0
. Since
d_{r}
is a metric on
\mathcal{P}(r)

(e.g.
Villani 2003
, Theorem 7.3)
, this yields
P=Q
, as required. In fact,
\bigl(\mathcal{P}(r),d_{r}\bigr)
is a separable, complete metric space
(e.g.
Panaretos and Zemel 2020
, Theorem 2.2.7 and Proposition 2.2.8)
, so by the equivalence (ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii) in Theorem
7.17
, the same is true of
\bigl(\mathcal{P}(r),\widetilde{d}_{r}\bigr)
.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): As in the proof of Proposition
7.16
, the function
f_{N}\colon x\mapsto(N-\|x\|)\vee 0\wedge 1
once again serves as a Lipschitz surrogate for the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N}}
of
B_{N}\equiv\bar{B}_{D}(0,N)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}:\|x\|\leq N\}
for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
in the argument below; note that
f_{N}=1
on
B_{N-1}
,
f_{N}=0
on
B_{N}^{c}
and
f_{N}
is 1-Lipschitz on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
. In view of this and the fact that
\psi_{r}\colon x\mapsto\|x\|+\|x\|^{r}
belongs to
\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
, the function
\psi_{r}(1-f_{N})
also lies in
\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N,m}
and
\mathcal{H}_{N,m}
be the finite sets constructed in the proof of Proposition
7.16
for each
N,m\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
T_{r}^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{N,m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{H}_{N,m}
. Since
T_{r}^{\prime}
is a set of bounded Lipschitz functions, we certainly have
T_{r}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
. We claim that
T_{r}:=T_{r}^{\prime}\cup\{\psi_{r}(1-f_{N}):N\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a countable subset of
\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
with the required property. To see this, suppose that (i) holds for this set
T_{r}
, i.e. that
P_{n}(\psi)\to P(\psi)
for all
\psi\in T_{r}
. As noted in (
202
), we have
\widetilde{d}_{r}(P_{n},P)=\sup_{\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)}\lvert(P_{n}-P)(\phi)\rvert
for all
n
, where
\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)
denotes the set of all
\phi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r,1)
satisfying
\phi(0)=0
, so it suffices to show that the latter quantity converges to 0.

We will consider a decomposition (
204
) similar to (
203
) in the proof of Proposition
7.16
, taking particular care in this instance to ensure that the subsequent bounds hold uniformly over
\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)
. Observe that since
\psi_{r}(1-f_{N})\to 0
pointwise on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
as
N\to\infty
, and
\psi_{r}(1-f_{N})
is dominated by the
P
-integrable function
\psi_{r}
on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
for each
N
, we have
P\bigl(\psi_{r}(1-f_{N})\bigr)\to 0
as
N\to\infty
by the dominated convergence theorem. Thus, for an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a sufficiently large
N\equiv N_{\varepsilon,r}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
P\bigl(\psi_{r}(1-f_{N})\bigr)<\varepsilon/4
, and we also fix
m\equiv m_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
1/m<\varepsilon/4
. With this choice of
N
and
m
, it follows from the defining property of
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N,m}
that for any
\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)
, there exists
\tilde{h}_{\phi}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N,m}
such that
\sup_{x\in B_{N}}|\phi(x)-\tilde{h}_{\phi}(x)|<1/m<\varepsilon/4
. Letting
h_{\phi}
be the corresponding function in
\mathcal{H}_{N,m}
as above, we have

\displaystyle\lvert(P_{n}-P)(\phi)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\bigl|(P_{n}-P)\bigl(\phi(1-f_{N})\bigr)\bigr|+\lvert(P_{n}-P)(\phi f_{N}-h_{\phi})\rvert+\lvert(P_{n}-P)(h_{\phi})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq(P_{n}+P)\bigl(\lvert\phi\rvert(1-f_{N})\bigr)+(P_{n}+P)(\lvert\phi f_{N}-h_{\phi}\rvert)+\max_{\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{N,m}}\lvert(P_{n}-P)(\psi)\rvert

(204)

by the triangle inequality. Now for every
\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)
, we have
\lvert\phi(x)\rvert=\lvert\phi(x)-\phi(0)\rvert\leq\|x\|+\|x\|^{r}=\psi_{r}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
. Since
P_{n}\bigl(\psi_{r}(1-f_{N})\bigr)\to P\bigl(\psi_{r}(1-f_{N})\bigr)
as
n\to\infty
by assumption, this implies that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)}(P_{n}+P)\bigl(\lvert\phi\rvert(1-f_{N})\bigr)\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,(P_{n}+P)\bigl(\psi_{r}(1-f_{N})\bigr)=2P\bigl(\psi_{r}(1-f_{N})\bigr)<\varepsilon/2.

(205)

Moreover, for any
\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)
, the functions
\phi f_{N}
and
h_{\phi}
are both supported on
B_{N}
, and
\lvert\phi f_{N}-h_{\phi}\rvert=|\phi-\tilde{h}_{\phi}|\,f_{N}\leq|\phi-\tilde{h}_{\phi}|<\varepsilon/4
on
B_{N}
, so

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)}(P_{n}+P)(\lvert\phi f_{N}-h_{\phi}\rvert)\leq 2\sup_{\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)}\sup_{x\in B_{N}}\,\lvert\phi(x)f_{N}(x)-h_{\phi}(x)\rvert<\varepsilon/2.

(206)

Finally, since
\mathcal{H}_{N,m}
is finite and
P_{n}(\psi)\to P(\psi)
for all
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{N,m}\subseteq T_{r}
by assumption, we have
\max_{\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{N,m}}\lvert(P_{n}-P)(\psi)\rvert\to 0
. Combining this with (
204
), (
205
) and (
206
), we conclude that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,\widetilde{d}_{r}(P_{n},P)=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,1)}\lvert(P_{n}-P)(\phi)\rvert<\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon/2=\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, the desired conclusion follows.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii): Suppose that
\widetilde{d}_{r}(P_{n},P)\rightarrow 0
and let
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
be a (bounded)
L
-Lipschitz function, for some
L>0
. Then
\tilde{\psi}(\cdot):=\psi(\cdot)/L\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r,1)
, so
P_{n}(\psi)=LP_{n}(\tilde{\psi})\rightarrow LP(\tilde{\psi})=P(\psi)
. Hence
P_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}P
. Moreover, the function
x\mapsto\|x\|^{r}
belongs to
\mathrm{PL}(r,(r/2)\vee 1)
since by Lemma
7.20
below,

\bigl|\|x\|^{r}-\|y\|^{r}\bigr|\leq\frac{r\vee 2}{2}\,\bigl|\|x\|-\|y\|\bigr|\bigl(\|x\|^{r-1}+\|y\|^{r-1}\bigr)\leq\frac{r\vee 2}{2}\|x-y\|\bigl(\|x\|^{r-1}+\|y\|^{r-1}\bigr)

(207)

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, so
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\|x\|^{r}\,dP_{n}(x)\rightarrow\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\|x\|^{r}\,dP(x)
. We conclude that
d_{r}(P_{n},P)\rightarrow 0
.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i): We will show here that if (iii) holds, then
P_{n}(\psi)\to P(\psi)
for all
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
. Indeed, suppose that
P_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}P
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\|x\|^{r}\,dP_{n}(x)\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\|x\|^{r}\,dP(x)
. Now for
L>0
and any
\psi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{PL}}_{D}(r,L)
, we have
\lvert\psi(x)\rvert\leq L\|x\|(1+\|x\|^{r-1})\leq 2L(1+\|x\|^{r})
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
. Thus, since
\psi
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
and
x\mapsto\lvert\psi(x)\rvert/(1+\|x\|^{r})
is bounded on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, it follows from (iii) and
Dümbgen et al. 2011
that
P_{n}(\psi)\to P(\psi)
.
∎

Remark 7.19
.

The proof of the implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) in Theorem
7.17
is similar to the argument in
Dudley 2002
showing that (b) implies (c) in his Theorem 11.3.3, where it is established that the bounded Lipschitz metric induces the topology of weak convergence (of probability measures on a separable metric space).

To obtain a sharp pseudo-Lipschitz constant for
x\mapsto\|x\|^{r}
in (
207
) above, we apply the following elementary inequality.

Lemma 7.20
.

If
a,b\geq 0
and
r\geq 1
, then
\lvert a^{r}-b^{r}\rvert\leq\max(1,r/2)\,\lvert a-b\rvert\,(a^{r-1}+b^{r-1})
.

Proof.

Suppose without loss of generality that
0\leq b\leq a
. If
r\geq 2
, then
t\mapsto rt^{r-1}
is convex on
[0,\infty)
, so

\displaystyle a^{r}-b^{r}=\int_{a}^{b}rt^{r-1}\,dt\leq\int_{a}^{b}r\left(\frac{t-b}{a-b}\,a^{r-1}+\frac{a-t}{a-b}\,b^{r-1}\right)dt=\frac{r}{2}(a-b)(a^{r-1}+b^{r-1}).

If
r\in[1,2]
, then
0\leq(ab)^{r-1}(a^{2-r}-b^{2-r})=ab^{r-1}-ba^{r-1}
, so
a^{r}-b^{r}\leq(a-b)(a^{r-1}+b^{r-1})
.
∎

When we have a sequence of possibly random probability measures
P_{n}\equiv P_{n}(\omega)
on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, we can apply the deterministic Theorem
7.17
to obtain Corollary
7.21
below, in which we equip
\mathcal{P}(r)
with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{r}\equiv\mathcal{B}\bigl(\mathcal{P}(r)\bigr)
associated with the
d_{r}
(or equivalently the
\widetilde{d}_{r}
) metric. Note that
\widetilde{d}_{r}(P_{n},P)
is measurable (i.e. a bona fide random variable) for each
n
by Proposition
7.16
. The measurability of
d_{r}(P_{n},P)
is guaranteed by
Villani 2009
; see also
Panaretos and Zemel 2020
.

Corollary 7.21
.

Fix
P\in\mathcal{P}(r)\equiv\mathcal{P}_{D}(r)
and let
(P_{n})
be a sequence of random elements
P_{n}\colon\Omega\to\mathcal{P}(r)
.

(a)

Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\psi\,dP_{n}\overset{a.s.}{\to}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\psi\,dP
for every
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
;

(ii)

\widetilde{d}_{r}(P_{n},P)\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
;

(iii)

d_{r}(P_{n},P)\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
.

(b)

The same equivalences hold if the mode of convergence in (i)–(iii) is instead taken to be either convergence in probability or complete convergence.

Thus, to establish the seemingly stronger conclusions in (ii) and (iii) for a random sequence of distributions
P_{n}
, a putative limit
P\in\mathcal{P}_{D}(r)
and any of the above modes of stochastic convergence, it is sufficient (and sometimes more convenient) to show that the appropriate version of (i) holds for each
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
in turn. This is the approach we take in the proofs of the master theorems for symmetric AMP (Theorems
2.1
and
2.3
).

Proof.

(a) The implications (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i) are immediate from Theorem
7.17
. As for (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii), note that for each
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
in (i), the event
\Omega(\psi)
of probability 1 on which
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\psi\,dP_{n}\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\psi\,dP
may depend (a priori) on
\psi
. The key point is that under (i), Theorem
7.17
ensures that this convergence is actually uniform over
\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r,1)
on a
countable
intersection of such events
\Omega(\psi)
. More precisely, letting
T_{r}\subseteq\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
be as in Theorem
7.17
, we see that
\bigcap_{\,\psi\in T_{r}}\Omega(\psi)
is an event of probability 1 on which (ii) and (iii) hold.

(b)
Convergence in probability
:

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): First, we prove that if
P_{n}(\psi)\overset{p}{\to}P(\psi)
for each
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
, then
\widetilde{d}_{r}(P_{n},P)\overset{p}{\to}0
, or equivalently that every subsequence of
\bigl(\widetilde{d}_{r}(P_{n},P):n\in\mathbb{N}\bigr)
has a further subsequence that converges almost surely to 0. It suffices to show that for any subsequence
(Q_{k})\equiv(P_{n_{k}})
, there is a further subsequence
(Q_{k_{\ell}})
such that with probability 1, we have
Q_{k_{\ell}}(\psi)\to P(\psi)
for all
\psi\in T_{r}\subseteq\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
; indeed, the desired conclusion then follows directly from (a). To this end, enumerate the elements of the countable set
T_{r}
as
\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\dotsc
and apply a diagonal argument: since
Q_{k}(\psi_{1})\overset{p}{\to}P(\psi_{1})
, we can extract a subsequence
(Q_{k_{1,\ell}})
of
(Q_{k})
such that
Q_{k_{1,\ell}}(\psi_{1})\overset{a.s.}{\to}P(\psi_{1})
as
\ell\to\infty
. Continuing inductively, we see that for each
J\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist a subsequence
(Q_{k_{J,\ell}})
of
(Q_{k_{J-1,\ell}})
and an event of probability 1 on which
Q_{k_{J,\ell}}(\psi_{j})\to P(\psi_{j})
as
\ell\to\infty
for all
1\leq j\leq J
. Finally, let
Q_{k_{\ell}}:=Q_{k_{\ell,\ell}}
for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, and observe that with probability 1, we have
Q_{k_{J,\ell}}(\psi_{j})\to P(\psi_{j})
as
\ell\to\infty
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, as required.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i): As above, we can argue along subsequences of
(P_{n})
and then appeal directly to the corresponding implications in (a).

Complete convergence
:

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): Suppose that
P_{n}(\psi)\overset{c}{\to}P(\psi)
for every
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
. In view of Definition
1.1
of complete convergence, it suffices to show that if
(\beta_{n})
is any sequence of random variables with
\beta_{n}\overset{d}{=}\widetilde{d}_{r}(P_{n},P)
for each
n
, then
\beta_{n}\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
. For any such sequence
(\beta_{n})
, we first seek to construct a sequence
\bigl(\tilde{P}_{n})
of random elements
\tilde{P}_{n}\colon\Omega\to\bigl(\mathcal{P}(r),\widetilde{d}_{r}\bigr)
such that
\tilde{P}_{n}\overset{d}{=}P_{n}
on
\bigl(\mathcal{P}(r),\mathcal{B}_{r}\bigr)
for each
n
and
\bigl(\widetilde{d}_{r}(\tilde{P}_{n},P):n\in\mathbb{N}\bigr)=(\beta_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N})
almost surely as random sequences. Since
\bigl(\mathcal{P}(r),\widetilde{d}_{r})
is a Polish space, a suitable
\bigl(\tilde{P}_{n})
can be obtained by applying Lemma
7.10
, where for each
n
, we take
g_{n}\colon\bigl(\mathcal{P}(r),\widetilde{d}_{r}\bigr)\to\mathbb{R}
to be the 1-Lipschitz (and hence Borel measurable) function
Q\mapsto\widetilde{d}_{r}(P,Q)
.

For each
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
, we see from the definition of
\widetilde{d}_{r}
in (
4
) that
Q\mapsto Q(\psi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\psi\,dQ
is also a 1-Lipschitz (and hence Borel measurable) function from
\bigl(\mathcal{P}(r),\widetilde{d}_{r}\bigr)
to
\mathbb{R}
, so
\tilde{P}_{n}(\psi)\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable (i.e. a random variable). Now
\tilde{P}_{n}\overset{d}{=}P_{n}
for each
n
by construction, so for every
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
, it follows that
\tilde{P}_{n}(\psi)\overset{d}{=}\tilde{P}_{n}(\psi)
for each
n
and hence that
\tilde{P}_{n}(\psi)\overset{a.s.}{\to}P(\psi)
. Thus, by the implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) in (a) above, we conclude that
\beta_{n}=\widetilde{d}_{r}(\tilde{P}_{n},P)\to 0
almost surely, as required.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i): To establish these remaining implications, observe that it suffices to show the following: if
F_{n},G_{n}\colon\mathcal{P}(r)\to\mathbb{R}
are Borel measurable functions for which it is known from (a) that
F_{n}(P_{n})\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
implies
G_{n}(P_{n})\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
, then
F_{n}(P_{n})\overset{c}{\to}0
implies
G_{n}(P_{n})\overset{c}{\to}0
. To prove this, we can proceed as in the argument for (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): given any random sequence
(\beta_{n})
such that
\beta_{n}\overset{d}{=}G_{n}(P_{n})
for each
n
, Lemma
7.10
yields a sequence
(\tilde{P}_{n})
of random elements
\tilde{P}_{n}\colon\Omega\to\mathcal{P}(r)
such that
F_{n}(\tilde{P}_{n})\overset{d}{=}F_{n}(P_{n})
and
\beta_{n}=G_{n}(\tilde{P}_{n})
almost surely for each
n
. Then
F_{n}(\tilde{P}_{n})\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
, so (a) implies that
\beta_{n}=G_{n}(\tilde{P}_{n})\to 0
almost surely. This completes the proof.
∎

We conclude this subsection with some straightforward results on pseudo-Lipschitz functions.

Lemma 7.22
.

For
D\in\mathbb{N}
, if
f\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r)
and
g\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(s)
for some
r,s\geq 1
, then
fg\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r+s)
and
\lvert f\rvert^{p}\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(pr)
for all
p\geq 1
.

Proof.

There exists
L>0
such that
f\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r,L)
and
g\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(s,L)
. Letting
L^{\prime}:=L\vee\lvert f(0)\rvert\vee\lvert g(0)\rvert
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert f(x)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert f(0)\rvert+\lvert f(x)-f(0)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq L^{\prime}(1+\|x\|+\|x\|^{r})\leq 2L^{\prime}(1+\|x\|^{r})

(208)

\displaystyle\lvert g(x)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert g(0)\rvert+\lvert g(x)-g(0)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq L^{\prime}(1+\|x\|+\|x\|^{s})\leq 2L^{\prime}(1+\|x\|^{s})

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
. Therefore, fixing arbitrary
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
and setting
a:=\|x\|\vee\|y\|
, we see that

\displaystyle\lvert f(x)g(x)-f(y)g(y)\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\lvert f(x)\rvert\,\lvert g(x)-g(y)\rvert+\lvert g(x)\rvert\,\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq 2L^{\prime}L\,\|x-y\|\,\bigl\{\bigl(1+\|x\|^{r}\bigr)\bigl(1+\|x\|^{s-1}+\|y\|^{s-1}\bigr)+\bigl(1+\|x\|^{s}\bigr)\bigl(1+\|x\|^{r-1}+\|y\|^{r-1}\bigr)\bigr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq 2L^{\prime}L\,\|x-y\|\,(2+2a^{r-1}+a^{r}+2a^{s-1}+a^{s}+4a^{r+s-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq 20L^{\prime}L\,\|x-y\|\,(1+a^{r+s-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq 20L^{\prime}L\,\|x-y\|\,(1+\|x\|^{r+s-1}+\|y\|^{r+s-1}).

This shows that
fg\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r+s)
. For
p\geq 1
, we have
(a+b)^{p-1}\leq(1\vee 2^{p-2})(a^{p-1}+b^{p-1})
for
a,b\geq 0
, and it follows from Lemma
7.20
and (
208
) that

\displaystyle\bigl|\lvert f(x)\rvert^{p}-\lvert f(y)\rvert^{p}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{p\vee 2}{2}\,\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert\,\bigl(\lvert f(x)\rvert^{p-1}+\lvert f(y)\rvert^{p-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L(2L^{\prime})^{p-1}(p\vee 2)}{2}\,\|x-y\|\,(1+\|x\|^{r}+\|y\|^{r})\bigl((1+\|x\|^{r})^{p-1}+(1+\|y\|^{r})^{p-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p}L(L^{\prime})^{p-1}\,\|x-y\|\,(1+\|x\|^{r}+\|y\|^{r})\bigl(1+\|x\|^{(p-1)r}+\|y\|^{(p-1)r}\bigr)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p}L(L^{\prime})^{p-1}\,\|x-y\|\,(1+\|x\|^{pr}+\|y\|^{pr})

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
. Thus,
\lvert f\rvert^{p}\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(pr)
, as required.
∎

Lemma 7.23
.

Let
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D+1}(r,L)
for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
,
r\geq 1
and
L>0
. Fix
c\equiv(c_{1},\dotsc,c_{D})\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
and
\tau>0
.

(a)

For fixed
x\equiv(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{D})\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, define
\psi_{x}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\psi_{x}(z):=\psi\bigl(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{D},\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}c_{\ell}\,x_{\ell}+\tau z\bigr)
. Then
\psi_{x}\in\mathrm{PL}_{1}(r,L_{\|x\|,\tau})
, where
L_{a,\tau}:=L\tau\max\{1+(2\vee 2^{r-1})(1+\|c\|)^{r-1}a^{r-1},(1\vee 2^{r-2})\tau^{r-1}\}
for
a\geq 0
.

(b)

Let
Z\sim N(0,1)
and define
\Psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\Psi(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{D}):=\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\psi\bigl(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{D},\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}c_{\ell}\,x_{\ell}+\tau Z\bigr)\bigr\}
. Then
\Psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r,L_{\tau})
, where
L_{\tau}:=L(1+\|c\|)\max\{1+(2\vee 2^{r-1})\,\mathbb{E}(\lvert\tau Z\rvert^{r-1}),(1\vee 2^{r-2})(1+\|c\|)^{r-1}\}
.

Proof.

For
x\equiv(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{D})\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
, note first that

\displaystyle\bigl\|\bigl(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{D},\textstyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}c_{\ell}\,x_{\ell}+\tau z\bigr)\bigr\|^{r-1}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(\|x\|+\lvert\textstyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}c_{\ell}\,x_{\ell}\rvert+\tau\lvert z\rvert\bigr)^{r-1}

\displaystyle\leq\{(1+\|c\|)\|x\|+\tau\lvert z\rvert\}^{r-1}

(209)

\displaystyle\leq(1\vee 2^{r-2})\bigl\{(1+\|c\|)^{r-1}\|x\|^{r-1}+\tau^{r-1}\lvert z\rvert^{r-1}\bigr\},

(210)

where the three bounds above are obtained using the triangle inequality, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the fact that
(a+b)^{r-1}\leq(1\vee 2^{r-2})(a^{r-1}+b^{r-1})
for
a,b\geq 0
.

(a) For
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\psi_{x}(z)-\psi_{x}(z^{\prime})\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\bigl|\psi\bigl(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{D},\textstyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}c_{\ell}\,x_{\ell}+\tau z\bigr)-\psi\bigl(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{D},\textstyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}c_{\ell}\,x_{\ell}+\tau z^{\prime}\bigr)\bigl|

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq L\tau\lvert z-z^{\prime}\rvert\,\bigl\{1+2(1\vee 2^{r-2})(1+\|c\|)^{r-1}\|x\|^{r-1}+(1\vee 2^{r-2})\,\tau^{r-1}\bigl(\lvert z\rvert^{r-1}+\lvert z^{\prime}\rvert^{r-1}\bigr)\bigr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq L_{\|x\|,\tau}\,\lvert z-z^{\prime}\rvert\,\bigl(1+\lvert z\rvert^{r-1}+\lvert z^{\prime}\rvert^{r-1}\bigr),

where the first bound follows from (
210
) and the fact that
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D+1}(r,L)
.

(b) For
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\Psi(x)-\Psi(y)\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl|\psi\bigl(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{D},\textstyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}c_{\ell}\,x_{\ell}+\tau Z\bigr)-\psi\bigl(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{D},\textstyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}c_{\ell}\,y_{\ell}+\tau Z\bigr)\bigr|\bigr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq L(1+\|c\|)\|x-y\|\,\bigl\{1+2(1\vee 2^{r-2})\,\mathbb{E}(\lvert\tau Z\rvert^{r-1})+(1\vee 2^{r-2})(1+\|c\|)^{r-1}\bigl(\|x\|^{r-1}+\|y\|^{r-1}\bigr)\bigr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq L_{\tau}\|x-y\|\,\bigl(1+\|x\|^{r-1}+\|y\|^{r-1}\bigr),

where the second bound again follows from (
209
), (
210
) and the fact that
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D+1}(r,L)
.
∎

Lemma 7.24
.

Suppose that
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r,L)
for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
,
r\in[2,\infty)
and
L>0
. Then for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and vectors
x^{\ell}\equiv(x_{1}^{\ell},\dotsc,x_{n}^{\ell})
and
y^{\ell}\equiv(y_{1}^{\ell},\dotsc,y_{n}^{\ell})
for
1\leq\ell\leq D
, we have

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\lvert\psi(x_{i}^{1},\dotsc,x_{i}^{D})-\psi(y_{i}^{1},\dotsc,y_{i}^{D})\rvert\leq LD^{\frac{r}{2}-1}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}\|x^{\ell}-y^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r}\right)^{1/r}\biggl(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}\,\bigl(\|x^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r-1}+\|y^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r-1}\bigr)\biggr).

Proof.

For
1\leq i\leq n
, define
X^{(i)}:=(x_{i}^{1},\dotsc,x_{i}^{D})
and
Y^{(i)}:=(y_{i}^{1},\dotsc,y_{i}^{D})
, and let
r^{\prime}:=r/(r-1)\in(1,2]
be the Hölder conjugate of
r
, so that
1/r+1/r^{\prime}=1
. Then since
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}_{D}(r,L)
, an application of Hölder’s inequality yields the bound

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\lvert\psi(x_{i}^{1},\dotsc,x_{i}^{D})-\psi(y_{i}^{1},\dotsc,y_{i}^{D})\rvert=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\lvert\psi(X^{(i)})-\psi(Y^{(i)})\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L\|X^{(i)}-Y^{(i)}\|\,\bigl(1+\|X^{(i)}\|^{r-1}+\|Y^{(i)}\|^{r-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\leq L\,\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|X^{(i)}-Y^{(i)}\|^{r}\biggr)^{1/r}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\bigl(1+\|X^{(i)}\|^{r-1}+\|Y^{(i)}\|^{r-1}\bigr)^{r^{\prime}}\biggr)^{1/r^{\prime}}.

(211)

Since
\|{\cdot}\|\equiv\|{\cdot}\|_{2}\leq D^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{r}}\|{\cdot}\|_{r}
on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, we see that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|X^{(i)}-Y^{(i)}\|^{r}\leq\frac{D^{\frac{r}{2}-1}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}\,\lvert x_{i}^{\ell}-y_{i}^{\ell}\rvert^{r}=D^{\frac{r}{2}-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}\|x^{\ell}-y^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r}.

(212)

In addition, by applying the triangle inequality for
\|{\cdot}\|_{n,r^{\prime}}
and arguing as in (
212
), we have

\displaystyle\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\bigl(1+\|X^{(i)}\|^{r-1}+\|Y^{(i)}\|^{r-1}\bigr)^{r^{\prime}}\biggr)^{1/r^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|X^{(i)}\|^{r}\biggr)^{1/r^{\prime}}+\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|Y^{(i)}\|^{r}\biggr)^{1/r^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\biggl(D^{\frac{r}{2}-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}\|x^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r}\biggr)^{\frac{r-1}{r}}+\biggl(D^{\frac{r}{2}-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}\|y^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r}\biggr)^{\frac{r-1}{r}}

\displaystyle\leq 1+(D^{\frac{r}{2}-1})^{\frac{r-1}{r}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{D}\,\bigl(\|x^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r-1}+\|y^{\ell}\|_{n,r}^{r-1}\bigr),

(213)

where the final bound follows since
\|{\cdot}\|_{r}\leq\|{\cdot}\|_{r-1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
. Combining (
211
)–(
213
) yields the desired conclusion.
∎
